{"id": "1B.10", "text": "Did the ruler leave a demonstrably safer and more durable existential-risk posture than inherited, accounting for authority, implementation, external shocks, and unresolved exposure?"}

GUIDE:
# Chapter 1B — Nuclear And Existential-Risk Responsibility

Status: **draft; v5 objective-evidence questions require controlled rejudgment**

## Chapter Identity

- Chapter: `1B`
- Rating: nuclear and global existential-risk responsibility
- Evidence strategy: local-first structured evidence plus cited manual research
- Rubric version: `chapter_1b_v5`
- Output: one chapter score (`1`–`10`) and one confidence assessment per ruler-period

## Purpose And Non-Goals

This chapter asks whether the ruler used the risk-bearing powers actually available
to them responsibly, reduced catastrophic danger, and left a safer posture. A high
score means prudent restraint, credible safeguards, good-faith risk reduction, and
responsible crisis behavior. A low score means reckless escalation, proliferation,
unsafe control, coercive nuclear shielding, or knowingly increased catastrophic risk.

It does not rank rulers by arsenal size alone, reward non-nuclear rulers automatically,
or penalize a ruler merely because they inherited a dangerous posture. Conventional
military strength, generic environmental policy, prestige, and peaceful rhetoric are
not substitutes for demonstrated existential-risk choices. Non-nuclear cyber,
biological, or AI policy belongs here only where a credible catastrophic pathway and
ruler-level choice are shown. The client matrix is never evidence.

## Ten Evidence Lenses

| Lens | Simple question | Detailed research question | Priority evidence |
|---|---|---|---|
| **1B.1 — Reducing existential risk** | Did the ruler try to make catastrophic conflict less likely? | Did the ruler use formal authority, strategy, directives, and resource choices to reduce nuclear or other existential risk rather than increase prestige, leverage, or personal power through escalation? | **Formal acts and law**; **Resources**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **1B.2 — Responsible nuclear rhetoric** | Did the ruler speak about nuclear weapons responsibly? | Did the ruler avoid reckless threats, brinkmanship, apocalyptic language, and normalization of nuclear use, and did formal doctrine, orders, and operational posture corroborate rather than contradict responsible rhetoric? | **Rhetoric and representations**; **Formal acts and law**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **1B.3 — Safe nuclear control** | Did the ruler keep nuclear weapons and decisions safe and controlled? | Did the ruler enact, fund, staff, implement, and enforce effective command-and-control, custody, safety, inspection, and accident-prevention safeguards, and correct identified failures? | **Resources**; **Personnel**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **1B.4 — Arms control and inspections** | Did the ruler support and follow agreements that reduce nuclear danger? | Did the ruler support, ratify, implement, fund, and comply with arms-control, inspection, nonproliferation, disarmament, and de-escalation agreements, or obstruct and weaken them? | **Formal acts and law**; **Resources**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **1B.5 — Nuclear cover for aggression** | Did the ruler avoid using nuclear power to protect aggression or repression? | Did the ruler avoid formally or operationally using nuclear capability to authorize, shield, or intensify conventional aggression, territorial coercion, or domestic repression? | **Formal acts and law**; **Rhetoric and representations**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **1B.6 — Preventing proliferation** | Did the ruler stop allies, clients, and domestic actors from spreading nuclear weapons? | Did the ruler establish and enforce proliferation controls against allies, proxies, clients, firms, and domestic factions, and respond when monitoring exposed evasion or assistance? | **Formal acts and law**; **Resources**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **1B.7 — Independent risk expertise** | Did the ruler empower qualified experts who could warn about catastrophic risks? | Did the ruler appoint qualified, independent experts, protect their access and dissent, and resource risk-reducing institutions rather than replace expertise with loyalty or ideology? | **Personnel**; **Resources**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **1B.8 — Crisis de-escalation** | During crises, did the ruler act to prevent catastrophic escalation? | In crises, did the ruler issue and implement de-escalatory decisions, preserve communication and decision safeguards, and correct procedures exposed as dangerous? | **Implementation and operational conduct**; **Rhetoric and representations**; **Formal acts and law** |
| **1B.9 — Other catastrophic risks** | Did the ruler manage AI, cyber, biological, and similar catastrophic risks carefully? | Did the ruler establish, fund, enforce, and transparently review precautionary legal and institutional safeguards for AI, cyber, biological, and other catastrophic dual-use risks? | **Formal acts and law**; **Resources**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |
| **1B.10 — Lasting risk posture** | Did the ruler leave the country’s catastrophic-risk posture safer than before? | Did the ruler leave a demonstrably safer and more durable existential-risk posture than inherited, accounting for authority, implementation, external shocks, and unresolved exposure? | **Outcomes**; **Implementation and operational conduct** |

Priority evidence categories indicate promising evidence, not required coverage or an
exclusive source list. Missing categories do not invalidate a lens.

## Researcher Guidance

Follow the local-first researcher guide. Start with the resolved ruler-period and local
D17/FAS facts, then check SIPRI and FAS narrative material. Search by themes rather
than issuing ten repetitive searches: inherited capability and authority; arsenal and
doctrine change; safeguards/command systems; treaties and inspections; threats and
crises; proliferation relationships; expert appointments; catastrophic dual-use policy;
and end-of-period posture.

Prefer IAEA, UNODA/UN treaty records, CTBTO, treaty inspection bodies, declassified or
contemporaneous official records, SIPRI/FAS technical assessments, legislative or
inquiry records, serious scholarship, and reputable reporting. Official claims about
restraint or safety need independent corroboration; official texts remain strong for
formal doctrine, appointments, treaty acts, and dated statements. Preserve contrary
evidence and uncertainty. Do not infer responsible rhetoric from silence or safety from
the absence of reported accidents.

Record reusable evidence once and point it to every materially relevant lens. Separate
target-year evidence, broader ruler-period evidence, inherited baseline, and later
retrospective evidence. The researcher describes evidence and attribution but does not
assign the final chapter score.

## Sparse, Historical, And Closed-Regime Evidence

For non-nuclear rulers with no plausible authority or exposure, document that limited
exposure; do not manufacture activity or award an automatic `10`. For older cases use
archives, treaty records, memoirs cautiously, cabinet/command histories, and later
scholarship, marking retrospective and contested claims. In closed regimes, missing
reporting is weak evidence: triangulate external monitoring, satellite/technical
assessments, defectors only with corroboration, and adversarial-source caveats. A thin
record may still support a cautious chapter score when the known exposure and choices
are clear; otherwise widen the confidence interval or flag review.

Classify exposure before scoring as `material_exposure`,
`limited_exposure_with_choices`, `no_discriminating_opportunity`, or
`unknown_due_to_evidence_gap`. The last category normally produces a null and a
targeted research continuation. `No_discriminating_opportunity` also produces a null:
it documents why the ruler cannot be discriminatingly scored and must not be ranked
against materially exposed rulers. Generic multilateralism,
civil nuclear phase-out, or the absence of nuclear rhetoric is contextual unless tied
to a ruler-attributed catastrophic-risk choice.

## Attribution And Inherited Baseline

Establish what authority the ruler personally held over doctrine, forces, diplomacy,
appointments, and crisis decisions. Quarantine acts of predecessors, military bodies,
coalition partners, or dominant patrons unless adoption, direction, knowing tolerance,
or failure to use available corrective authority is evidenced. Judge change from the
inherited posture alongside absolute risk: inheriting a huge arsenal is not itself
culpability, while expanding or recklessly using it is; prudent reduction deserves
credit even when the remaining stockpile is large.

## Chapter-Level 1–10 Rubric

| Score | Chapter anchor |
|---:|---|
| 1 | The ruler authorized or caused an actual nuclear detonation against people or a military target, or deliberately caused an equivalent realized nuclear catastrophe. Actual use establishes eligibility for the floor; context, target, scale, alternatives, and restraint still determine whether 1 is warranted. |
| 2 | The ruler moved credibly toward nuclear use or created an acute near-use danger through operational preparation, direct brinkmanship, extremely unsafe command, or sustained nuclear coercion during war, without a realized nuclear detonation. |
| 3 | Severe nuclear risk increase through doctrine expansion, deployment, proliferation, repeated threats, safeguard abandonment, or destabilizing exercises, but without demonstrated near-use. |
| 4–5 | Mixed or weak responsibility: meaningful restraint in some areas but consequential escalation, obstruction, unsafe governance, or deterioration in others. |
| 6–7 | Generally responsible risk management and some tangible risk reduction, with notable omissions, ambiguity, inherited constraints, or inconsistent conduct. |
| 8–9 | Strong, sustained, and consequential restraint, safeguards, diplomacy, expert governance, and crisis prudence that materially improved the posture. |
| 10 | Exceptional, durable, and well-evidenced leadership that substantially reduced catastrophic risk across the ruler's actual sphere of authority while resisting powerful contrary incentives. |

The lenses are qualitatively and non-equally weighted. Crisis escalation, actual
weapon use, proliferation, and command safety can dominate several weaker signals.
No arithmetic average, equal ten-percent weighting, or minimum lens count is required.
Exposure matters: the judge evaluates available choices, consequences, and authority.
Scoring is opportunity-adjusted. Limited exposure or authority is not automatically a
high or low score. A ruler may earn a high score when meaningful risk-bearing choices
arose and the ruler consistently exercised responsible restraint within their actual
authority. When no discriminating opportunity exists, return null with an explicit
exposure assessment; do not manufacture excellence, blame, or a midpoint from mere
non-exposure.

Scores of 6 or above require a direct ruler-attributed risk-bearing choice. Scores of
8 or above additionally require consequential improvement or restraint supported by
multiple independent source types. Country posture or silence alone cannot support a
score.

### Absolute-scale guardrails

The annual cohort does not define the endpoints. The worst ruler in a current batch
must not receive 1 unless the realized-use floor is met. Nuclear threats, coercive
shielding of conventional war, doctrine hardening, exercises, deployments, or arsenal
growth can be profoundly irresponsible while remaining in bands 2 or 3. Classify each
record before scoring as `realized_use_or_catastrophe`,
`credible_near_use_or_operational_preparation`,
`severe_escalation_without_near_use`, `mixed_risk_management`,
`generally_responsible_management`, `strong_consequential_risk_reduction`, or
`exceptional_durable_risk_reduction`.

A rationale may not describe threats, exercises, deployments, or doctrine change as
"maximally dangerous" merely because no worse case appears in the annual cohort. Since
no nuclear weapon has been detonated in war after 1945, a modern annual batch can
properly contain no score of 1.

The general historical-extreme analogy (including references to Nazi-regime-level
conduct) does not replace this chapter-specific test. Regime cruelty, repression, or
aggression alone cannot establish the 1B floor: the ruler must meet the realized nuclear
use or equivalent realized-catastrophe condition above.

## Judge Workflow Across Rulers

### V4 direct-evidence and absolute-floor gate

A numeric score requires a target-period choice, action, restraint, remedy, or
outcome within the ruler's actual nuclear or existential-risk authority. Generic
nuclear posture, inherited arsenals, speeches, and pre- or post-period treaties are
context only. If no such target-period fact exists, return null; do not manufacture
a neutral or favorable score from low exposure.

One Chapter 1B judge applies this rubric across all eligible rulers in the comparison
batch. The judge first inventories exposure and inherited posture, reads every dossier,
identifies common anchors and edge cases, provisionally orders rulers, then assigns one
chapter score and confidence to each. It must explain the decisive evidence, meaningful
counterevidence, attribution, change from baseline, and why adjacent higher/lower bands
were rejected. Revisit the full ordering after scoring; use overlapping anchor cases if
context limits require shards. Preserve citations and flag research gaps without
turning formatting differences into substantive failures.

## Minimum Evidence And Graceful Degradation

A score needs at least one traceable, relevant source and a stated structured prior or
`not_available`, plus an explicit exposure/authority and temporal-fit assessment.
High-confidence extreme scores normally require multiple independent source types and
evidence of consequential conduct. Sparse lenses, unknown appointments, or unavailable
internal control details lower confidence rather than mechanically lower the score.
If identity, core attribution, or citation authenticity is unresolved, withhold the
score for review; ordinary incomplete coverage is not a publication blocker.

## Common Evidence-Item Semantics

Each material evidence item should carry a stable evidence ID; a concise factual claim;
source URL, title, publisher, and publication/access date where available; source type
and confidence with a short reason; a quotation or precise locator when available;
target-year/ruler-period fit; ruler attribution and inherited-baseline relevance; its
positive, negative, mixed, contextual, or contrary role; and the supported lens IDs.
Fields may be normalized later and natural-language equivalents are acceptable. The
substantive minimum is a traceable source, intelligible claim, temporal fit, attribution,
and evidence role. One item may support multiple lenses without duplication.

## Common Chapter-Judgment Output Envelope

The judge returns one object per ruler with:

- `chapter_id`
- `rubric_version`
- `calibration_batch_id` and `calibrated_against`
- `score_1_to_10`, or null with `insufficient_evidence_reason`
- `confidence_score` and `plausible_score_range`
- `decisive_positive_evidence` and `decisive_negative_evidence`
- `inherited_baseline_and_constraints` and `ruler_attribution`
- `supported_lenses` and `missing_or_weak_lenses`
- `contrary_evidence`
- `source_mix` and `structured_prior_summary`
- `chapter_rationale`
- `lower_anchor_rejected` and `higher_anchor_rejected`
- `manual_review_required`, `manual_review_reason_type`, and `manual_review_reason`

Exact field serialization can be repaired cheaply after judgment; missing harmless
formatting must not erase a substantively usable assessment.

## Bias And Comparability Checks

Check visibility and secrecy, nuclear/non-nuclear exposure, great-power attention,
survivorship and hindsight, adversarial intelligence claims, rhetoric-versus-capability,
population/state-size irrelevance, inherited arsenals, crisis opportunity, source type,
recency, and consistent cross-ruler calibration. Do not equate report volume with risk,
treat a treaty signature as implementation, or reward a ruler for lacking capacity they
never sought or controlled.

## Chapter-Specific Calibration Fields

Alongside common calibration metadata, record:

- `chapter_rubric_version`: `chapter_1b_v5`
- `calibration_batch_id` and `calibrated_against`
- `exposure_profile`: nuclear authority, latent/dual-use exposure, crisis exposure
- `inherited_risk_posture` and `end_posture`
- `ruler_authority_and_attribution`
- `decisive_risk_increasing_evidence`
- `decisive_risk_reducing_evidence`
- `crisis_and_command_safety_assessment`
- `source_mix`, `information_environment`, and `period_fit`
- `lens_strengths`, `lens_gaps`, and `contrary_evidence`
- `score_rationale`, `lower_anchor_rejected`, `higher_anchor_rejected`
- `confidence_score` (`0`–`100`) and `confidence_rationale`

The generic abuse-oriented calibration enums may be `not_applicable` with explanation;
chapter-specific fields carry the substantive calibration.

## Smoke-Test Ruler Set

| Ruler | Country | Year / period | Calibration role |
|---|---|---:|---|
| Mikhail Gorbachev | Soviet Union | 1985–1991 | major inherited arsenal; arms-control improvement |
| John F. Kennedy | United States | 1962 | severe crisis and de-escalation evidence |
| Nikita Khrushchev | Soviet Union | 1962 | severe crisis, mixed escalation/de-escalation |
| Ronald Reagan | United States | 1981–1989 | rhetoric-to-arms-control mixed trajectory |
| Vladimir Putin | Russia | 2022 | nuclear shielding and threat rhetoric low anchor |
| Kim Jong Un | North Korea | 2012–2023 | proliferation, testing, closed information environment |
| F. W. de Klerk | South Africa | 1989–1994 | voluntary disarmament edge/high case |
| Indira Gandhi | India | 1974 | nuclear-test decision and historical attribution case |
| Jacinda Ardern | New Zealand | 2017–2023 | non-nuclear/limited-exposure edge case |
| Boris Yeltsin | Russia | 1991–1999 | inherited-control and institutional-capacity edge |

## Draft Acceptance Checklist

- Exact texts for all ten registry lenses are present.
- Research uses local priors and cited underlying sources.
- Evidence is reusable across lenses and contrary evidence is preserved.
- One judge calibrates one chapter score across a varied ruler batch.
- Missing lenses affect confidence, not validity or a mechanical score.
- Attribution, inherited posture, secrecy, and exposure are explicit.
- Adjacent-anchor explanations are substantive.
- Smoke testing and independent review occur before activation.

The Corrective Action Programme (CAP) started in 2004, which required the\n               integration of the Corrective Action Programme Group (CAPG). This group\n               began to coordinate the events assessments with increasing emphasis on Root\n               Cause Analysis. Since 2012 the “Tap Root” methodology has been continuously\n               utilized to analyse the events classified as Regulatory Level 1 and Level 2 (Root\n               Cause Analysis). Currently, at least 2 of the following methodologies are used:\n               Task Analysis; Cause and Effect Analysis; Event and Causal Factor Chart Analysis;\n               Tap Root Analysis; Fault Tree Analysis; Change Analysis; Barrier Analysis and\n               Human Performance Evaluation. Tel: 50006000   www.gob.mx/sener               Period 2019-2021\n                                                            6-6\n\nUNITED MEXICAN STATES\n                                                                                NATIONAL REPORT\n\n\n\n        Strategies for Beyond Design-Basis External Events\", EA-12-050 (EA-13-109) \"Order to\n        Modify Licenses with regard to Reliable Hardened Containment Vents\" and EA-12-051\n        \"Order to Modify Licenses with regard to Reliable Spent Fuel Pool Instrumentation\".\n\n        To address conditions resulting from events beyond the design basis, CFE\n        implemented the mitigation strategies indicated in 10 CFR 50.54 (hh)(2) complying\n        with the NEI 06-12 Rev.2 \"B.5.b Phase 2 & 3 Submittal Guideline\". In this way, the LVNPS\n        has developed strategies for inventory makeup and spray to the Spent Fuel Pool (SFP),\n        primary containment spray, inventory makeup in the condenser hot well and\n        Condensate Storage Tanks (CST), inventory makeup to the reactor vessel derived from\n        events with extensive damage, through a portable submersible pump powered by a\n        diesel engine and various sources of makeup water. Additionally, following the\n        guidelines of this NEI 06-12, strategies were developed to depressurize the reactor\n        vessel, maximize the flow of the Control Rod Drive System (CRD), manually isolate the\n        Reactor Water Clean-Up System (RWCU), manually vent the Primary Containment in\n        case of lack of direct current (DC) and control leaks in the Spent Fuel Pool (SFP) in\n        extreme situations derived from events beyond the design basis.\n\n        For Order EA-12-049 \"Mitigation Strategies for Beyond Design Basis External Events \"\n        required by CNSNS, CFE successfully implemented Phases 1 and 2 of the FLEX\n        Strategies in accordance with NEI 12-06 \"Diverse and Flexible Coping Strategies (Flex)\n        Implementation Guide\". As part of the FLEX Strategies, LVNPS made modifications to\n        connect the Alternate Building Diesel Generators and Portable Diesel Generators to\n        direct current (DC) battery chargers. This was done to address an Extended Loss of AC\n        Power (ELAP) event, thus maintaining the key functions of core cooling, Spent Fuel\n        Pool cooling, and primary containment integrity. In addition, modifications were\n        made to facilitate hose connections to the Residual Heat Removal and High Pressure\n        Core Spray systems to maintain core cooling through a portable diesel pump and\n        multiple makeup water sources.\n\n        For the USNRC Order EA-12-050 (EA-13-109), as required by CNSNS, installed a reliable\n        hardened containment venting system for Phase 1, in accordance with NEI Guide 13-\n        02 \"Guidance for Compliance with Order EA-13-109\". For Phase 2 of Order EA-13-109,\n        LVNPS developed the Severe Accident Water Addition and Management\n        (SAWA/SAWM) methodology, following the guidelines of NEI 13-02.\n\n        In accordance with CNSNS requirements for Order EA-12-051 \"Reliable Spent Fuel Pool\n        Instrumentation\", CFE completed the modification packages on the Diversification of\n        the Spent Fuel Pool Instrumentation at both LVNPS units. This instrumentation is\n        capable of reliably monitoring the Reliable Spent Fuel Pool parameters (level and\n        temperature) during Extended Energy Loss and during accident conditions.\n\n        CFE has developed the Emergency Severe Accident Guidelines (ESAG) from the BWR\n        Reactor Owners Group (BWROG) Emergency Procedure Guidelines/Severe Accident\n        Guidelines Rev. 4 (EPG/SAG). The GEAS coordinate the control of key plant parameters\n        under severe accident conditions, and provide guidelines for submerging the core\n        and its slag. In addition, support procedures have been developed for the ESAG, which\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Tel: 50006000   www.gob.mx/sener             Period 2019-2021\n                                                            6-7\n\nUNITED MEXICAN STATES\n                                                                                NATIONAL REPORT\n\n\n\n        contain the information to perform the necessary actions to carry out the alignments,\n        connections and disconnections required for ESAG monitoring.\n\n        In addition to the ESAG, LVNPS developed the Emergency Management Guide (EMG)\n        based on BWROG documents; this guide serves as a reference for prioritizing available\n        resources and response strategies in the event of an extensive damage event, by\n        coordinating emergency response groups and external support groups.\n\n        In conclusion, the overall progress of the Post Fukushima Actions is 98% and is\n        scheduled to be fully completed by the end of 2023.\n\n   -    Identification of the installations that were required to shutdown\n        Currently, there are no identified conditions that might adversely affect safe\n        operation; therefore, there are no plans to prematurely shut down the LVNPS\n        installation before the end of its authorized lifetime.\n\n   -    A statement on the Contracting Party position regarding the continued\n        operation of nuclear installations, including those that do not comply with\n        the obligations stated in Articles 10 - 19 of the Convention; explaining how\n        safety and other issues were taken into account to achieve this position\n        Based on the information presented in this and other articles of the National Report\n        prepared to meet the commitments under the Convention on Nuclear Safety; it is\n        concluded that Mexico has laws, regulations and means for adequate inspection and\n        supervision by its regulatory body - independent from the exploitation organisms -\n        which ensures that LVNPS operation does not represent an undue risk to public\n        health and safety nor the environment.\n\n        Regarding the specific compliance with the obligations derived from the Convention\n        on Nuclear Safety, the progress and achievements during the reporting period are\n        presented in each of the subsequent articles.\n\n        The conclusion from the existing objective evidence, a summary of which is presented\n        in this report, is that LVNPS continues maintaining a safety performance level similar\n        to other nuclear plants operating in countries with more nuclear experience.\n        Currently, there are no identified conditions that might adversely affect safe\n        operation; therefore, there are no plans to prematurely shut down the installation\n        before the end of its authorized lifetime.\n\n\n\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. PRIORITY TO SAFETY\n\n        Obligations\n\n        \"Each Contracting Party shall take the appropriate steps to ensure that all\n        organisations engaged in activities directly related to nuclear installations shall\n        establish policies that give due priority to nuclear safety.\"\n\n   -    Overview of the Contracting Party’s arrangements and regulatory\n        requirements regarding policies and programmes to be used by the licence\n        holder to prioritize safety in activities for design, construction and\n        operation of nuclear installations, including:\n\n       Description of the agreements and regulatory requirements\n\n        Regarding the agreements and regulatory requirements to give priority to safety,\n        Articles 19, 20, 21, 28, 32, 34, and 50 of the Regulatory Law on Nuclear Matters of Article\n        27 of the Constitution establishes that of all installation activities performed by the\n        installation owner, including planning, design, construction and operation as well as\n        definitive shutdown and decommissioning, safety always has the highest level of\n        importance to achieve the safety objectives. Likewise, the Regulatory Body has\n        established the conditions under which it will monitor the compliance of nuclear,\n        radiological and physical safety standards and safeguards to ensure that the\n        operation of the facilities is carried out with maximum safety.\n\n        For the renewal of LVNPS Authorisation of Operation, extended to 30 years, the\n        Regulatory Body updated the conditions and requirements linked to this\n        authorisation, in order to monitor and maintain the commitment to apply the highest\n        safety standards. These conditions and requirements establish circumstances and\n        deadlines for the delivery of reports and record keeping, as well as conditions that\n        would lead to a suspension, cancellation or revocation of the authorisation of\n        operation, emphasizing the principles of Safety Culture.\n\n       Safety policies\n\n        Laguna Verde Nuclear Power Station’s (LVNPS) safety policy is shown in Figure 10.1a\n        and it is based on the Traits for a Healthy Nuclear Safety culture from Institute of\n        Nuclear Power Operations’ (INPO) document INPO 12-12 Rev. 1 of April 1, 2013. These\n        traits and associated attributes described are soundly based on significant events of\n        the nuclear industry. These principles and attributes are connected with, and\n        influence, the values, ownership, experiences, behaviours, beliefs and models of\n        LVNPS’s staff.\n\n        Likewise, the CFE's General Director, together with the General Secretary of the CFE’s\n        Union established the Nuclear Safety Culture Policy, shown in Figure 10.1b below.\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX.                                  NATIONAL REPORT\n\n\n\n        LVNPS Top Management is committed to the traits and policies for its daily duties.\n        Any difference or deviation is used as a basis for improvement.\n\n       Safety culture programmes and development\n\n        The Safety Culture (SC) programme includes the following activities:\n\n\n             Establishment of the Safety Culture Policy.\n             Designation of a group of Safety Culture Ambassadors for the continuous\n              monitoring of Safety Culture, through the collection of cultural events.\n             Perform quarterly reports of continuous monitoring of Safety Culture from the 4th\n              Quarter of 2016.\n             Semi-annual meetings with the LTS (Leadership Team in Site [Staff of the CFE’s\n              Nuclear Corporate Coordination]) issuing and a report, and follow-up actions.\n             Development of promotional material.\n             Weekly reinforcement of the Traits of a Healthy Culture of Nuclear Safety (THCNS)\n              conducted by the organisation’s leaders:\n              o      Presentation in the supervisors meetings.\n              o      Presentation by supervisors in workshops.\n            SC self-assessment program following the guidelines provided by IAEA during\n             Safety Culture seminars, under the “Enhancing Nuclear Power Plant Life\n             Management and Safety Culture Practices in Latin America” project\n             (RLA9080/RLA9083/RLA9089), which are established in the internal procedure\n             \"Guide to conduct a Nuclear Safety Culture Self-Assessment\" This methodology is\n             based on the combination of the following techniques:\n              o      Surveys.\n              o      Interviews.\n              o      Focus groups.\n              o      Documental review.\n              o      Field observations.\n             Feedback to staff about the results of the SC self-assessment.\n             Annual survey of Safety Culture.\n             Evaluation of the THCNS involved in CFE’s nuclear corporate coordination (NCC)\n              events that require a root cause analysis.\n        In addition to the Safety Culture programme, LVNPS has implemented a set of\n        essential processes, which were developed to create a sustainable framework for\n        Safety Culture. The SC framework is shown in Figure 10.2. The Safety Culture pyramid\n\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. As an example, in Figure 10.3 the results for the fourth quarter of 2021 are\n        displayed. Performance Indicators for radiation protection, industrial safety and\n        environmental protection have also been developed.\n\n        The self-assessment process is being used by LVNPS’s groups to identify opportunities\n        for improvement. The process has improved significantly, both in quantity and quality\n        of self-assessments. For instance, in the year 2018, 64 self-assessments were\n        conducted in LVNPS; eight were “Fast” self-assessment, 37 focused in specific topics\n        and 19 were “Follow-up” on topics evaluated in the period 2016 and 2017. Moreover,\n\n\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. The results from these evaluations are presented in\n        Figure 10.4.\n\n       Independent safety assessments\n\n        Currently, LVNPS is subject to two types of external assessments.\n\n        a)     The first one, in compliance with the Operation Licence and in accordance with\n               the Technical Specifications (TS) requirement, is an independent inspection to\n               the fire protection and loss prevention system, which is performed at least every\n               12 months.\n\n               These inspections can be performed by Federal Electricity Commission’s (CFE)\n               qualified personnel, external inspectors or by staff of a company specialized in\n               fire protection or quality assurance. If CFE’s qualified personnel are chosen, then\n               at least every three years the inspection and the audit must be performed by\n               staff of a company specialized in the topics mentioned above.\n\n        b)     The second type corresponds to external assessments that go beyond regulatory\n               requirements, such as International Atomic Energy Agency (IAEA) Assessment\n               of Safety Significant Events Team (ASSET), Operational Safety Assessment\n               Review Team (OSART) and Safety Culture Assessment Review Team (SCART)\n               missions, as well as peer or counterpart reviews, carried out by WANO, because\n               LVNPS is a member of this association since 1997.\n\n\n        Table 10.1 presents the external assessments conducted from 1997 to December 2020.\n\n                                  Assessment                               Date\n                       WANO (Peer review)                      November 1999\n                       TUV ANLAGENTECHNIK                      November 2000 to\n                       (Independent audit)                     January 2001\n                       WANO (Peer review)                      December 2002\n                       WANO (Peer review)                      December 2004\n                       WANO (Peer review)                      September 2006\n                       WANO (Peer review)                      February 2009\n                       SCART                                   November 2009\n\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. This multidisciplinary team of Safety Culture\n        Ambassadors are responsible for safety culture self-assessments, and to provide all\n        the required information to the organisation to implement the improvement\n        activities needed to positively influence the culture of the NCC.\n\n        In 2016 and 2018 the last peer reviews Peer Review were carried out at the Laguna\n        Verde Nuclear Power Plant by WANO Atlanta Center, through which the performance\n        of the plant was evaluated according to the latest revision of the \"Objectives and\n        Performance Criteria for WANO peer reviews\" (2013). The last WANO Peer Review was conducted in\n        October 2020.\n\n       Discussion on measures to improve safety culture\n\n        LVNPS is the first Latin American centre to implement the IAEA’s Safety Culture\n        Continuous Improvement Process (SCCIP), under the project RLA9080 “Enhancing\n        Nuclear Power Plant Life Management and Safety Culture Practices in Latin America”.\n\n        With safety performance a priority, the evaluation and understanding of the safety\n        culture at the nuclear power plants are key elements to identify and prevent\n        vulnerabilities that can lead to a decrease in safety performance, as well as production\n        performance.\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Tel: 50006000   www.gob.mx/sener                     Period 2019-2021\n                                                           10-5\n\nUNITED MEXICAN STATES\n                                                                                NATIONAL REPORT\n\n\n\n        The process of IAEA´s continuous improving of safety culture, contributes to\n        strengthening and improving the safety culture of the entire organisation. The\n        process consists of five steps:\n\n           1.   Workshop on safety culture for managers, the purpose of which is to show how\n                the safety culture influences the safety performance, and to understand that a\n                culture change requires a big effort, more so in the managers than in the rest of\n                the organisation.\n           2. Theoretical and practical training of a team called the Safety Culture\n              Ambassadors, whose purpose is to perform self-assessments of the safety\n              culture and to promote change in the safety culture.\n           3. Complete a self-assessment of the safety culture with the purpose of obtaining\n              a diagnostic of the organisation that helps define improvement actions.\n           4. Dissemination of results and feedback from the organisation in order to finalize\n              the report.\n           5. Implement the improvement actions.\n\n        After working through the entire process, steps 3, 4 and 5 will periodically be repeated\n        in the CFE´s NCC (every three years) in order to achieve continuous improvement\n        of the safety culture and to contribute to the safety performance of the plant.\n\n        Additionally, LVNPS has incorporated the practices described in NEI 09-07 Rev. 1,\n        “Fostering a Healthy Nuclear Safety Culture,” providing the continuous monitoring\n        and with a higher frequency than the described in the IAEA model, permitting closer\n        monitoring of behaviour and prompt course correction if the desired results are not\n        being obtained.\n\n        This model is implemented through the procedure named \"Guide to establish a\n        process of continuous monitoring and supervision of Nuclear Safety Culture\", for\n        which the Panel for Monitoring Safety Culture (PMSC) formed by the Safety Culture\n        Ambassadors (monthly meetings) and the Leadership Team in Site (LTS) formed by\n        the CFE´s NCC Staff (semi-annual meetings) and based on these meetings,\n        decisions are taken that reinforce the actions taken or redirect them.\n\n       A process-oriented (quality) management system\n\n        LVNPS developed a Quality Management System (QMS), which consists of a set of\n        documents such as: the quality policy, the organisation manual, processes manual,\n        comprehensive management system plan, work procedures, quality records, and\n        training programmes.\n\n        The QMS has been certified by a company accredited in International Quality\n        Certifications by the Mexican Accreditation Authority (EMA) and audited internally\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Tel: 50006000   www.gob.mx/sener             Period 2019-2021\n                                                           10-6\n\nUNITED MEXICAN STATES\n                                                                                NATIONAL REPORT\n\n\n\n        according to the following standards: (1) ISO 9001:2015 / NMX-CC-9001-IMNC-2015\n        \"Quality Management Systems\", (2) ISO 14001:2015 / NMX-SAA-14001-IMNC-2015\n        \"Environmental Protection Management System\", and (3) ISO 45001:2018 / NMX-SAST-\n        001-IMNC-2018 \"Occupational Health and Safety.\"\n\n   -    Measures taken by the licence holder to implement arrangements for the\n        priority of safety, such as those above and any other voluntary activities,\n        examples of Good Practices and safety culture achievements;\n\n        The actions and good practices that CFE’s NCC has implemented to improve the\n        Safety Culture in its organisation are listed below.\n\n        1.    Workshops on Safety Culture\n\n              The object of these workshops is to reinforce the values to maintain an\n              organisational Safety Culture through the following:\n\n              a)      Stressing a high respect for nuclear technology.\n              b)      Increasing awareness, understanding and applying the Defence in Depth\n                      (DID) concept.\n              c)      Encouraging the development and implementation of Good Practices for\n                      Work and Operation.\n\n        2.    Safety Culture Monitoring Process\n\n              The guideline to establish the monitoring process and continuous supervision of\n              Nuclear Safety Culture, contains the basis for the monitoring process and\n              continuous supervision of the LVNPS’s SC health. The monitoring process is\n              shown in Figure 10.5 and works as follows:\n\n              The Nuclear Safety Culture Monitoring Panel (NSCMP) composed of Safety\n              Culture Ambassadors, monitors the process inputs through the recollection of\n              cultural events which are indicative of the organisation’s SC health to identify\n              strengths and potential problems that deserve further attention by the\n              organisation.\n\n              The NSCMP is composed of CFE´s NCC professionals from administrative and\n              technical areas (unionized and non-unionized personnel) with backgrounds in\n              Safety Culture. This group prepare quarterly reports and, in conjunction with its\n              Coordinator assigned by the CFE´s NCC, inform the Leadership Team in Site the\n              results of its continuous Monitoring. A report is generated from this meeting and\n              improvement actions are also established:\n\n\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Tel: 50006000   www.gob.mx/sener             Period 2019-2021\n                                                           10-7\n\nUNITED MEXICAN STATES\n                                                                                NATIONAL REPORT\n\n\n\n              The Nuclear Safety Culture Monitoring Panel has the following major functions:\n\n                  o   Continuous monitoring of the Nuclear Safety Culture of the CFE´s NCC\n                      (writing cultural events), maintaining a database.\n                  o   Form the Monitoring Panel of the Nuclear Safety Culture of the CFE´s\n                      NCC (review of cultural events and making quarterly reports).\n                  o   Present results of the Monitoring Panel of the Nuclear Safety Culture to the\n                      Leadership Team in Site of the CFE´s NCC, preparing a report every six-\n                      month.\n                  o   Carry out the annual survey of Nuclear Safety Culture of the CFE´s NCC,\n                      issuing a final report.\n                  o   Carry out the Nuclear Safety Culture CFE´s NCC self-assessment every 3\n                      years.\n\n        3.    Safety Culture Self-Assessment Process\n\n              In the procedure named \"Guide to conduct a Nuclear Safety Culture Self-\n              Assessment\", the methodology provided by the IAEA is established, based on the\n              combination of the following techniques: Surveys, Interviews, Focus groups,\n              Documentary review and Field observations. Through the collection of cultural\n              events, cultural issues are established to obtain the drivers of Culture and, finally,\n              determine the basic assumptions. The result is to show an image of the culture of\n              the organization in which a report is made, which is disseminated to the\n              organization and actions are established to strengthen the organization's Safety\n              Culture.\n\n        4.    Assessment of LVNPS staff perceptions on Safety Culture\n\n              The surveys that CFE has conducted since 1998, changed in 2000 and 2006. In\n              these instances, a brief questionnaire to measure the level of staff awareness\n              regarding LVNPS Safety Culture was used. The most recent change was made in\n              2012 to update the survey according to the Utilities Service Alliance’s “Nuclear\n              Safety Culture Assessment Process Manual” (also cited in point 3 above), which\n              provides guidelines for the Safety Culture self-assessment process in the USA. The\n              new survey enables an objective comparison of the results, which allows\n              reorienting the allocation of resources to the areas that need improvement, and\n              provides the means to verify the effectiveness of the actions taken.\n\n        5.    Implementation and continuous improvement of Safety Culture\n\n              Several relevant actions have been implemented to provide continuous\n              improvement on Safety Culture issues, as described previously in the “Safety\n              culture programmes and development” section of this article.\n\n\n\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Tel: 50006000   www.gob.mx/sener              Period 2019-2021\n                                                           10-8\n\nUNITED MEXICAN STATES\n                                                                                NATIONAL REPORT\n\n\n\n   -    Regulatory processes for monitoring and oversight of arrangements used\n        by the licence holder to prioritize safety\n\n        The Reactor Oversight Process measures the safety significance of Safety\n        Performance Indicators, Findings issued during nuclear facility inspections and Self-\n        Evident Findings (Reportable Events); and determines their impact on the seven (7)\n        Safety Fundamentals of the Oversight model. Additionally, for those cases in which\n        some of the entries do not directly affect any of these seven (7) Fundamentals, there\n        is a second evaluation that considers the impact on safety in the so-called \"Transversal\n        Areas\". The Transversal Areas consider additional aspects to the technical\n        considerations and definitions of the seven (7) Safety Fundamentals considering the\n        items of: Human Performance, Problem Identification and Resolution and Safety\n        Culture. With the results of this evaluation, the CNSNS Basic Inspection Program is fed\n        back to address during the inspections, not only technical aspects but also issues\n        related to Human Factors within which the measurement of aspects related to the\n        Safety Culture of the facility is implicit.\n\n        Another opportunity for measurement at the facility is during the conduct of the\n        Corrective Action program inspection, as inspectors can review self-assessments of\n        the licensee's safety culture. If the licensee's performance declines, the inspectors\n        through a special inspection shall verify that the licensee's assessment, the scope of\n        the condition and the scope of the responses of the findings, which are important to\n        the risk, adequately considered the attributes of the safety culture. The above, in order\n        to determine the weaknesses of the safety culture and encourage the licensee to take\n        appropriate action before significant degradation of performance occurs and\n        independently conduct an assessment of the licensee's safety culture.\n\n        It is important to clarify that, using the existing Reactor Oversight Process framework,\n        safety culture oversight activities will be based on a step-by-step, open,\n        understandable, objective, risk-informed and performance-based approach. These\n        activities may range from requesting a safety culture self-assessment to a meeting\n        between licensee management levels to discuss licensee performance issues and\n        actions to resolve persistent and ongoing cross-cutting safety culture issues.\n\n   -    Means used by the regulatory body to prioritize safety in its own activities\n\n        CNSNS ultimate mission is to protect the health and safety of the population and the\n        environment. This objective extends throughout the organization as a top priority, at\n        all levels of responsibility, from the General Management to the operational levels.\n\n        Through its quality policy, CNSNS is committed to continuous improvement in order\n        to maintain international standards of nuclear, radiological and security and\n        safeguards in the peaceful use of nuclear energy. In accordance with the above, and\n        with safety as one of its strategic objectives, CNSNS senior management has adopted\n        as a supreme guiding principle to prioritize nuclear, radiological and security and\n        safeguards of nuclear and radioactive facilities in its decision-making process.\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Tel: 50006000   www.gob.mx/sener             Period 2019-2021\n                                                           10-9\n\nUNITED MEXICAN STATES\n                                                                                NATIONAL REPORT\n\n\n\n        During the period covered by this National Report, the CNSNS updated its \"Code of\n        Conduct for Public Servants of the Commission on Nuclear Safety and Safeguards” to\n        integrate the safety culture into the standards of behaviour and performance of its\n        personnel. This document states that CNSNS personnel put into practice the\n        principles and attributes of a safety-oriented organizational culture, promoting, as a\n        supreme priority, safety over any operational aspect of nuclear and radioactive\n        facilities to protect the health of the population and the environment.\n\n        The following safety culture principles were incorporated into the Code of Conduct,\n        among its Rules of Integrity and Values:\n\n        1.   Leadership for safety must be demonstrated at all levels of the organization.\n\n        2. All personnel of the organization have the individual and public responsibility to\n           exhibit behaviours that set an example to follow in terms of safety matter.\n\n        3. The culture of the organization promotes safety and encourages cooperation and\n           open communication.\n\n        4. The implementation of an integral safety perspective in the organization is\n           consolidated by working in a systematized way.\n\n        5. Continuous improvement, learning and self-assessment are promoted at all levels\n           of the organization.\n\n\n        Also, with the participation of IAEA experts, in July 2019, the \"Safety Culture Self-\n        Assessment Workshop for the Regulatory Body\" was held in order to begin the\n        formation of a group of leaders in safety culture. To continue with the formation of\n        this group, arrangements were made with the IAEA to coordinate the \"Nuclear Safety\n        Workshop for Assessors\" at our facilities, to be held during the year 2022.\n\n\n\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. The objective of this requirement is that the LVNPS staff playing a vital\n        role in the efficient and safe generation of electric power (e.g., operators and\n        maintenance personnel) operate, verify and reset the plant equipment and its\n        components in such a way that the causal factors of human error are eliminated as\n        much as possible.\n\n        Consistent with this requirement, a general guideline was also established that in\n        order to comply with 10 CFR 50.34(f)(2)(iii), LVNPS should follow the directions of the\n        Standard Review Plan Chapter 18, NUREG-0800 (USNRC, 2004), which in combination\n        with the Human Factors Engineering Programme Review Model described in\n        NUREG-0711, provide detailed criteria to be considered in a Human Factors\n        Programme.\n\n        As a result of the implementation of all tasks required in the Standard Review Plan\n        Chapter 18, the CNSNS reviewed the LVNPS compliance with the human factors\n        engineering principles. Likewise, the simulator shall be\n               equipped with the Safety Parameters Display System.\n        b)     Requirement No. 6 establishes that LVNPS must continue with the Alarm\n               Reduction Programme in the Main Control Room, implementing the necessary\n               design changes.\n        In the case of Unit 1, its Operating Permit, renewed until 2050, no longer explicitly\n        describes the above, as this is embedded in compliance with Condition No. 4, which\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Continue the practices suggested in the “Human Factors Engineering Program\n               Review Model” described in NUREG-0711 whose recommendations can and\n               must be applied during the design, construction and operation of nuclear\n               installations.\n\n               According to this review model, the inclusion of the Human Factors Engineering\n               principles was verified in several CFE activities by reviewing the following items:\n\n               a.     Operational experience - CNSNS inspections systematically consider\n                      operational experience, as does review of the condition reports, in which\n                      the involvement of operational experience is documented. In addition, an\n                      evaluation of operational experience, as established in the basic inspection\n                      programme, is performed every two years. During these activities the\n                      External and Internal Operational Experience evaluation and feedback\n                      process is reviewed.\n               b.     Man-machine interface design - During examinations for new operator\n                      licences and renewals for Reactor Operators (RO) and Senior Reactor\n                      Operator (SRO), the man-machine interface was continually evaluated in\n                      order to identify human error precursors caused by the new controls and\n                      operator aids, and to make changes in the Main Control Room if necessary.\n               c.     Training Programme - CNSNS continually follows the analysis,\n                      development, implementation, evaluation, and feedback processes of the\n                      Systematic Approach to Training. This approach considers the knowledge\n                      and skills that the staff must have to safely perform a task.\n        2.     The root cause analysis of operational events has improved the identification of\n               causal factors of human error, such as: environmental conditions; interface with\n               equipment design or condition; verbal and written communication; training\n               methods and qualification of personnel; planning and work practices;\n               supervision methods; and administrative methods. The above has allowed to\n               fully identify the factors that originated the human error.\n\n\n        Regarding the inspection program, as mentioned in Article 7, during the period\n        covered by this National Report, no inspections were carried out on activities related\n        to human and organizational factors at the Laguna Verde Nuclear Power Plant Units\n        1 and 2. The\n        training evaluation is done as follows.\n\n        a)     Theoretical training\n\n               At the end of the training session, the trainees are evaluated by a written\n               examination to demonstrate that the necessary knowledge to carry out the\n\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. The first is a “supervised practice,” during\n               which trainees (staff) perform activities based on a planned scenario, making use\n               of the resources available to them. The training supervisor can stop the activity\n               to make observations and to show how the function should be performed.\n\n               In the second stage, carried out without the interaction with the supervisor, the\n               trainees are required to perform an activity, one that is part of the emergency\n               plan, for an accident scenario that is unknown to them until the session begins.\n               They need to use all available material resources to perform the activity, and each\n               trainee is evaluated according to his or her performance, which must be in\n               adherence to procedures and the operations manual, specifically taking into\n               account the critical steps to satisfactorily perform the activity.\n\n        Both the theoretical and practical training are evaluated following the LVNPS internal\n        procedures.\n\n        Main results\n\n        During the 2019-2021 period, the Said exercise\n        was evaluated by the CNSNS, identifying 78 findings to the various Task Forces that\n        compose it, without these representing a risk to the population during the response\n        to a General Emergency at the plant. Currently, the Task Forces are working on the\n        implementation of the modifications, and carrying out the relevant training, prior to\n        the integrated exercise that was planned for 2021; however, the exercise was\n        rescheduled for 2022 at the request of the CFE and the National Civil Protection\n        Coordination.\n\n   -    International arrangements, including those with neighbouring States, as\n        necessary.\n\n        See Annexes 7.1 and 7.2 of this National Report.\n\nArticle 16 (2)        Information to the public and neighbouring states\n\n   -    Overview of the Contracting Party’s arrangements for informing the public\n        in the vicinity of the nuclear installations about emergency planning and\n        emergency situations\n\n        One of the key factors for a successful implementation of the measures to protect the\n        population living in a radius of up to 16 km from LVNPS is the information regarding\n        their participation in the event of a radiological emergency.\n\n        The main information media are the following:\n\n        a)     The EREP calendar, which is distributed in all homes and businesses located in a\n               radius of up to 16 km from LVNPS. The calendar has information on actions to be\n               completed by the population in case of an emergency. This information is\n               delivered to about 4,000 households.\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Tel: 50006000   www.gob.mx/sener             Period 2019-2021\n                                                           16-22\n\nUNITED MEXICAN STATES\n                                                                                NATIONAL REPORT\n\n\n\n        b)     Visits of communities to the LVNPS Information Centre. The Information Centre\n               makes the invitation and provides transportation for the community groups. The\n               communities have the opportunity to go to the centre and receive information\n               about the External Radiological Emergency Plan. This programme receives an\n               average of 300 people per year.\n        c)     Visits to schools around LVNPS. Over the past three years, information about\n               LVNPS and the EREP has been shared with more than 1,700 students.\n\n\n   -    Arrangements to inform competent authorities in neighbouring States, as\n        necessary\n\n        Since 1988, Mexico has been a part of the “Convention on Early Notification of a\n        Nuclear Accident” and the “Convention on Assistance in the Case of a Nuclear\n        Accident or Radiological Emergency”. Based on these conventions, there is an\n        established procedure to declare a radiological emergency at a Mexican nuclear\n        installation if an event has the likelihood of affecting neighbouring states. The\n        procedure’s objective, to provide early notification, and its application are the\n        responsibility of CNSNS. Notwithstanding the preceding, Mexico has bilateral\n        agreements for exchanging technical information (including the occurrence of\n        significant events) with their closest neighbours.\n\n        Mexico is also part of the IAEA Early Communications International System, which\n        would be used to immediately provide information to the IAEA in the event of a\n        radiological emergency at LVNPS. The same information would be available to\n        neighbouring states. Mexico is also a signatory of the “Vienna Convention on Civil\n        Liability for Nuclear Damage”. It is important to mention that the LVNPS site is over\n        500 km away from the border of the nearest country.\n\n        Mexico has a National System for Civil Protection. In the event of a radiological\n        emergency at a nuclear installation in Unites States of America, near the border with\n        Mexico, with the likelihood to affect the national territory, the DN-III Plan would be\n        activated. The Ministry of Defence is responsible to apply the DN-III Plan under the\n        direction of the Secretariat of the Interior, assisted by the Secretariat of Health and\n        DICONSA. The DN-III Plan includes the infrastructure required to establish adequate\n        communications, evacuation capacity and establishment of control points for taking\n        the appropriate actions during the emergency. However, it should be noted that the\n        United States nuclear power plants are sited more than 100 km from Mexico’s border\n        and other Central American countries such, as Guatemala, do not have this kind of\n        power stations.\n\n        Technical issues to be considered\n\n   -    Related to the Event at Japan’s Fukushima Daiichi Plant\n\n        In connection with the event that occurred at the Fukushima Daiichi plant in Japan\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Based on this assumption, the LVNPS developed strategies in which\n                     the response capacity is not compromised by the failure of any common\n                     system and/or equipment.\n\n              2)    Additional responsibilities of government agencies\n\n                     As mentioned before in this Article 16, with regard to the External\n                     Radiological Emergency Plan, the Organization for Emergencies Response\n                     is formed by the federal and state agencies listed previously, which form the\n                     External Radiological Emergency Plan Committee.\n\n              3)    New procedures\n\n                     LVNPS has developed the Severe Accident Management Guidelines (SAMG)\n                     based on the guidelines for the Emergency Procedures and for Severe\n                     Accidents EPG / SAG (Emergency Procedure Guidelines / Severe Accident\n                     Guidelines) Rev. 3 of the BWR Reactor Owners Group. The SAMGs coordinate\n                     the control of the key parameters of the plant under severe accident\n                     conditions, and provide the guidelines to flood the core and its slag.\n\n                     Additionally, support procedures (PDS) have been developed for the SAMGs,\n                     which contain the information to perform the necessary actions to carry out\n                     the alignments, connections and disconnections required for the monitoring\n                     of the SAMGs.\n\n              4)    Joint actions with various agencies and improvements in international\n                    cooperation\n\n                     Mexico, through CNSNS, participates in the Organisation for Economic Co-\n                     operation and Development’s (OECD) Nuclear Energy Agency (NEA) Senior\n                     Task Force on the “Impact of the Fukushima Daiichi Nuclear Plant Accident”.\n\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Tel: 50006000   www.gob.mx/sener             Period 2019-2021\n                                                           16-24\n\nUNITED MEXICAN STATES\n                                                                                  NATIONAL REPORT\n\n\n\n                     CNSNS is also a member of the Ibero-American Forum of Radiological and\n                     Nuclear Regulatory Agencies (FORO in Spanish), which has developed as one\n                     of its tasks, stress tests for nuclear power plants. The tests are similar to the\n                     ones defined by the European Nuclear Safety Regulators Group (ESREG) and\n                     the Western European Nuclear Regulators Association (WENRA).\n\n                     Regarding cooperation with international groups on this issue, the CFE is an\n                     active committee member within the BWR Reactor Owners Group\n                     (BWROG). This membership allows CFE to be prepared for events beyond\n                     the design basis in accordance with the fleet of reactors of the same type.\n                     The CFE´s interaction with committees such as the Fukushima Response\n                     Committee (FRC) and the Emergency Procedures Committee (EPC)\n                     facilitates the integrated response within the industry to the orders issued\n                     by the USNRC and endorsed by the CNSNS. Additionally, the interaction with\n                     plants of the same type allows the exchange of experiences with nuclear\n                     power plants that have already implemented the orders of the USNRC EA-\n                     12-049, EA-12-051 and EA-13-109, which have been required to theCNSNS.\n\n        Actions of the Regulatory Body\n\n        As a consequence of the accident at the Fukushima Daiichi nuclear power plant in\n        Japan, the CNSNS required the LVNPS to implement the necessary mitigation\n        strategies to comply with NRC Order EA-12-049 (Flex) during the initial, transition and\n        final response phases, to address beyond design basis accidents; as well as, the\n        development and implementation of procedures and guidelines for CFE's response\n        to these accidents.\n\n        In response to the above, CFE acquired additional equipment and material to comply\n        with the Order and reevaluated the areas established as Response Centers, in order\n        to guarantee the protection of the members of the emergency response team, due\n        to the leakage of radioactive material.\n\n        As a result, the following actions were taken at the LVNPS:\n\n        1.    The location for the storage of materials and equipment necessary for CFE's\n              emergency response was determined, which corresponds to the Flex Building,\n              which complies with the characteristics required by Order EA-12-049.\n        2.    A new Technical Support Center (CAT, Abbreviation in Spanish), called \"Alternate\n              CAT\" was assigned for the Internal Emergency Plan (IEP), located in the Integral\n              Process Information System building, which will be used in the event that the\n              support centres of both units of the LVNPS are disabled.\n        3.    The amount of potassium iodide tablets needed was reevaluated in order to have\n              a sufficient reserve for the emergency response personnel and the population\n              living within the 16 km zone, in case radiological prophylaxis is required.\n\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX.
