Their continued operation will generate more spent\n        fuel than originally planned, requiring that spent fuel continue to be stored in the\n        spent fuel pools (SFP).\n\n        Because the SFP were not designed with sufficient capacity to store the spent fuel\n        that will be generated during the plant’s useful life, now considering the Extended\n        Power Uprate (EPU) modification and the Renewal of Operating Authorisation for\n        both units, the need to augment the facilities capacity for spent fuel storage until it is\n        moved to a permanent disposal site has emerged. Therefore, in 2015-2016 an\n        Independent Spent Fuel Storage Installation (ISFSI) was built at LVNPS site.\n\n        The purpose of building this facility is to provide LVNPS with the installed capacity of\n        spent fuel storage, and to recover the storage capacity of both LVNPS units. Then, it\n        will be carried out “campaigns” in which spent fuel assemblies of the SFP of both units\n        are removed and confined in dry storage systems.\n\n        LVNPS successfully completed its Pre-Operational Testing and, as the cut-off date of\n        this National Report, has already hosted five dry storage casks at the ISFSI, containing\n        445 spent fuel assemblies. The facility's capacity will allow the storage of up to 130\n        storage systems, which would translate into 11,570 spent fuel assemblies, thus\n        allowing the plant to operate for up to 60 years.\n\n   -    Implementation of on-site treatment, conditioning and storage of\n        radioactive waste\n\n        Liquid waste treatment\n\n        The liquid waste collected comes from different sources, so they are divided as follows:\n        equipment drains, floor drains, regeneration and chemical drains, detergents and\n        laundry drains, and miscellaneous drains.\n\n        The treatment process is carried out in batches, maintaining surveillance and control\n        of the chemical quality through analysis and sampling. The specific actions depend\n        upon the two following activities:\n\n        a)     For recycling, LVNPS procedures set the chemical parameters that need to be\n               complied with.\n        b)     For release to the environment, the radioactive material is analysed to ensure\n               that any discharge is As Low As Reasonably Achievable (ALARA) and in\n               conformance with the requirements specified in 10 CFR 20 and 10 CFR 50,\n               including the dose design objective specified in 10 CFR 50 Appendix I.\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Tel: 50006000   www.gob.mx/sener             Period 2019-2021\n                                                           19-36\n\nUNITED MEXICAN STATES\n                                                                                NATIONAL REPORT\n\n\n\n        Likewise, with the aim of continuous improvement, in 2008 the \"Reverse Osmosis\"\n        system is put into operation to improve radioactive waste processes. The filtering\n        system, with the use of backwash flow filters (BFF), coal beds, ion exchange resins,\n        Ultra Filtration (UF) and two stages of reverse osmosis, allows LVNPS to treat all high\n        conductivity liquid wastes and high activity that are collected in the Radioactive and\n        Purification Waste Buildings. The above with the purpose of obtaining water with\n        chemical quality that allows it to be re-used in the water-steam cycle.\n\n        Additionally, in the years 2017-2018, the installation of a new Alternate System for the\n        Treatment of High Conductivity and Activity Liquid Waste (ATREX / G60) or High\n        Conductivity Waste Processing (HCWP) System was carried out in the Radioactive\n        Waste Building, Elevation 25.10, with the objective of providing greater processing\n        efficiency of high conductivity and activity wastes. The system has the capacity to\n        process the contributions to the Chemical Waste Concentrator (CWC) System in order\n        to achieve effluents with suitable chemical characteristics to be processed through\n        the Radioactive Floor Drain System (FDR) for liquid radioactive waste, of the Reverse\n        Osmosis System, radioactive distillate filters (MWR) or be discharged into the sea\n        when the chemical and radiological characteristics allow it.\n\n        Gaseous waste treatment\n\n        The gaseous treatment systems are designed according to the origin and radiation\n        levels expected in the following LVNPS extraction and ventilating systems:\n        Radioactive Gaseous (Off-Gas) Waste Treatment System, the Turbine Gland Seal\n        System and Ventilation Systems of different process buildings of the installation.\n\n        It has been verified that the off-gas treatment system is designed to collect and delay\n        exhaust of noble gases produced by fission, which are removed from the condenser\n        by means of steam jet ejectors. The noble gas process continues through a heater, a\n        hydrogen recombiner, a condenser, two 10-minute retention tanks, a pre-filter system,\n        activated carbon beds and high-efficiency air filters, and finally, through a ventilating\n        air current, which is monitored before being sent to the atmosphere.\n\n        This hydrogen recombiner system is designed to support postulated hydrogen\n        (generated by radiolysis) explosions. It has alarms that actuate if a high concentration\n        is detected, which alert the Main Control Room operator to isolate the faulty\n        recombiner and put the redundant train into service.\n\n        Environmental dosimetry studies performed considering LVNPS in operation have\n        demonstrated that design basis assumptions of gas treatment systems are adequate.\n        Based on the above, it is considered that gas treatment satisfies its function of limiting\n        the release of radioactive material in a gaseous form.\n\n\n\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Tel: 50006000   www.gob.mx/sener             Period 2019-2021\n                                                           19-37\n\nUNITED MEXICAN STATES\n                                                                                NATIONAL REPORT\n\n\n\n        Solid waste treatment\n\n        This subsystem is designed to collect and process the humid and dry solid waste\n        generated at LVNPS to confine them safely and reliable inside containers appropriate\n        for its eventual isolation. These can be stored in steel containers or high-density\n        polyethylene high-integrity containers (HICs).\n\n        The solid waste processing is controlled by the guidelines of the LVNPS Process\n        Control Programme (PCP), which complies with the Technical Specifications,\n        according to 10 CFR 61 and compliant with the standard NOM-019-NUCL-1995 (see\n        Annexe 7.3 of this National Report).\n\n        The wet solid wastes are treated by the cyclical draining process inside a high-\n        integrity container where the free water content is reduced to less than 1% by volume.\n\n        The dry solid wastes can be treated by one of the following processes:\n\n        a.     For the compactable waste, hydraulic compressing with a 4:1 compacting ratio.\n        b.     For non-compactable waste, direct packaging.\n\n        The sources of wet solid waste are: spent ion exchange resins, sludge of the phase\n        separators, concentrated chemical laboratory waste, regeneration and\n        decontamination solutions. The sources of dry solid waste include: cartridge filters,\n        clothing, paper, plastics, thermal insulation, sand, grit and contaminated replacement\n        components.\n\n        The drums and high-integrity containers with high-level radioactive waste already\n        processed coming from both LVNPS units are initially stored in the decay area, within\n        the Radioactive Waste Building. Later the drums and containers are transferred to the\n        Temporary On-site Storage, which is located in the facility site.\n\n        The polyethylene or steel-shell polyethylene HICs and the steel drums are\n        manufactured with radiation resistance, structural strength, and corrosion resistance,\n        and are consistent with the requirements for seismic design and quality assurance.\n        LVNPS has three areas for the temporary storage of radioactive solid waste:\n\n        1.     In-plant temporary storage (ATP, abbreviation in Spanish). It is inside the\n               Radioactive Waste Building, with a storage capacity for HICs and steel drums of\n               55 gallons, for wet and dry solid waste for two years of waste generation.\n        2.     On-site Temporary Storage (ATS, abbreviation in Spanish). It is located outside\n               the double fence area but on-site. This storage has two warehouses; Warehouse\n               I stores HICs and steel drums of 55 gallons for wet solid waste and is completely\n               full. Warehouse II stores only HICs from the operation of both LVNPS units.\n\n\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Tel: 50006000   www.gob.mx/sener             Period 2019-2021\n                                                           19-38\n\nUNITED MEXICAN STATES\n                                                                                NATIONAL REPORT\n\n\n\n        3.     Dry Solid Radioactive Waste Repository (DDRSS, abbreviation in Spanish). It is\n               outside the double fence but on-site, to store steel drums of 55 gallons and\n               metallic containers B25, B12 or special shape for dry solid waste.\n\n        During LVNPS Unit 1 start-up, it was decided to design and build an On-site\n        Temporary Storage installation for wet low and medium radioactive waste; since there\n        was no final repository site. This On-site Temporary Storage was authorized as an\n        extension of the storage area of the Radioactive Waste Treatment Building. This\n        installation was constructed in 1991, with an initial storage capacity for 5 years of\n        operation of LVNPS in both units, considering the original design output of waste.\n        Based on the LVNPS policy for waste reduction, the waste treatment process was\n        improved; and the storage area was restructured, so its capacity was optimised,\n        resulting in an increased storage capacity to accommodate waste up to 2007. The\n        LVNPS wet solid waste storage installed capacity has increased with the construction\n        of the industrial Warehouse II at the On-site Temporary Storage, which is currently in\n        operation.\n\n        Also, a “Dry Solid Radioactive Waste Repository” was enabled and CNSNS granted the\n        authorisation in 1993 to have sufficient storage capacity for dry radioactive waste,\n        which provided an optimised capacity through 2015, assuming a production similar\n        to current volumes. In 2006, studies for the implementation of additional volume\n        reduction methods were started to increase the storage capacity; since the storage is\n        about to be full. In 2007, a super-compaction process was carried out, which reduced\n        the volume by a factor of approximately 3:1 of 6,000 compactable solid waste drums.\n\n        As part of the continuous improvement effort, CFE installed a subsystem of the\n        reverse osmosis system, which consists of a dryer and granulator (currently in\n        standby) that is available to serve both LVNPS units and replaces the evaporators-\n        extruders that have operated for 15 years (which nowadays are inoperable and out of\n        service). In addition, the subsystem technology reduces the final waste compacting\n        ratio to 4:1. With these actions, CFE intends to maintain a reliable waste solidification\n        and reduce the wet solid waste volume. The purpose of this subsystem is to treat the\n        concentrated liquid waste generated during the different stages of the filtering\n        process and the concentrates produced by the waste evaporator system, in order to\n        obtain a dry solid waste with a moisture of less than 1 %. The dry solid waste will be\n        packed in 55-gallon drums for its final disposal.\n\n        Spent Fuel Storage\n\n        The ISFSI is designed to store spent fuel from the commercial operation of LVNPS\n        Units 1 and 2, taking into account the life extension resulting from the Renewal of\n        Operating Authorisation of 30 years. Its purpose is to store the spent fuel assemblies\n        in a safe and reliable manner in suitable containers, which subsequently can be used\n        during transportation to the final disposal site. The HI-STORM FW container system\n        consists of a multi-purpose container (MPC) within an outer casing made of steel and\n        concrete. The outer casing has penetrations in the bottom to allow air circulation to\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Tel: 50006000   www.gob.mx/sener             Period 2019-2021\n                                                           19-39\n\nUNITED MEXICAN STATES\n                                                                                NATIONAL REPORT\n\n\n\n        cool the MPC, and therefore, the spent fuel contained within it. In addition, the MPC\n        has a thermal siphon capacity that allows cooling of the fuel assemblies through the\n        flow of helium in the container. The MPC consists of a basket that holds the fuel\n        assemblies. The basket is made of neutron-absorbing material called Metamic-HT,\n        with properties that ensure control of temperature, reactivity, and structural\n        performance.\n\n        The spent fuel storage complies with the technical specifications for the ISFSI, 10 CFR\n        72, and the standards NOM-004-NUCL-1994 and NOM-035-NUCL-2000.\n\n   -    Activities to keep the amount of waste generated to the minimum\n        practicable for the process concerned, in terms of both activity and volume\n\n        In relation to LVNPS radioactive waste generation minimising processes, several\n        strategies are being put in place by CFE, including:\n\n        1.     Separation of radioactive material and more efficient, cleaner and expeditious\n               decontamination of tools and objects contaminated, in new separation and\n               decontamination workshops.\n        2.     For compactable solid radioactive waste, super-compacting and fragmenting;\n               for not compactable waste, selective decontamination mainly of metallic waste.\n        3.     Optimising the use of materials to maintain equipment, tools and items free of\n               contamination.\n\n\n   -    Established procedures for clearance of radioactive waste\n\n        CFE has a Technical Manual for the Process Control Programme, which is mandatory\n        as prescribed by Technical Specification 3/4.11.3. The manual objective is to ensure that\n        the treatment and conditioning processes are carried out for all the different types of\n        low-level solid radioactive waste generated at LVNPS, such as: spent resins, temporary\n        resins, precoat sludge, sludge from cleaning, sludge concentrates, activated carbon,\n        active dry and other wastes.\n\n   -    Regulatory review and control activities\n\n        As mentioned in Article 14, CNSNS verifies annually the Radioactive Waste\n        Management through its Baseline Inspection Programme. In particular, the\n        inspection verifies the radioactive waste confinement, stability and control at LVNPS\n        installations dedicated to these activities.\n\n        Additionally, during the 2019-2021 period, relating to radioactive waste management,\n        the activities presented below were carried out.\n\n\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Tel: 50006000   www.gob.mx/sener             Period 2019-2021\n                                                           19-40\n\nUNITED MEXICAN STATES\n                                                                                NATIONAL REPORT\n\n\n\n        LVNPS storages for radioactive contaminated materials (AMAC)\n\n        Due to the implementation of the Extended Power Uprate (EPU) project in both\n        LVNPS units, in 2007 the Federal Electricity Commission (CFE) initiated the\n        procedures for the construction authorisation of the Storages for Radioactive\n        Contaminated Materials (SRCMs, named AMAC in Spanish) for the temporary storage\n        of dry solid radiologically contaminated materials that resulted from the\n        implementation of the aforementioned project. Of the five AMAC storages proposed,\n        only two were built and authorized by the CNSNS.\n\n        Subsequently, CFE requested CNSNS that the SRCMs could also temporarily store dry\n        solid materials, radiologically contaminated free of transferable contamination, which\n        were generated in LVNPS during its normal operation, refuelling outages and special\n        projects.\n\n        In December 2017, CFE requested CNSNS to extend the operation validity of SRCM\n        storages. Since the authorisation for the operation of these storages would expire on\n        December 31, 2018, in addition, the CFE requested in 2018 that CNSNS authorize the\n        storage of residues with dry solid radioactive material in the SRCMs.\n\n        Finally, in November 2020, the Authorisation to extend the Operating Term of the\n        Storages for Radioactive Contaminated Materials, SRCM 1 and 2, of the LVNPS was\n        granted, as well as an amendment to said authorisation to include the storage of\n        waste with dry solid radioactive material.\n\n        LVNPS storage for radioactive contaminated materials called “4 Naves”\n\n        CFE informed CNSNS that had the necessity for sufficient space for temporary and\n        safe storing of metal containers type B12, B25, ISO and other special forms for the\n        temporary storage of radioactively contaminated dry solid materials, free from\n        transferable contamination, which were generated at LVNPS during normal\n        operation, refuelling outages, and special projects. In the year 2010, CFE requested\n        CNSNS an authorisation for the reconditioning and operation of one LVNPS Storage\n        for Radioactive Contaminated Materials called \"4 Naves\" as a warehouse for this type\n        of containers. The operating authorisation granted for the \"4 Naves\" warehouse\n        expired on December 31, 2018.\n\n        The CFE had planned to carry out the management of the materials stored there\n        during the years 2019 and 2020, so at the end of 2018 requested the CNSNS to grant\n        the authorisation to cease of operations for this warehouse. Additionally, the CFE\n        requested the CNSNS authorisation to store dry solid radioactive material. This\n        request was subsequently cancelled by the CFE in January 2021. In September 2021\n        the CFE informed that the management of the materials stored in the \"4 Naves\"\n        warehouse will conclude in December 2022.\n\n\n\nAvenida de los Insurgentes Sur #890, Col del Valle\nAlcaldía Benito Juárez, CP. 03100 CDMX. Attacks exposed six terabytes of sensitive military information, disrupted transport licensing and training, and affected refinery operations; deficiencies included obsolete systems, missing patches, weak risk assessment, inadequate monitoring, and failure to correct known vulnerabilities.", "question_ids": ["1B.10", "1B.3", "1B.9", "8B.5", "8B.6", "8B.8"], "polarity": "adverse", "period_fit": "The article was published in November 2022 and describes failures during López Obrador's administration relevant to the 2023 posture.", "ruler_attribution": "The failures occurred in federal institutions under López Obrador's government; personal knowledge or direction is not established.", "limitations": ["The source does not document whether all cited weaknesses remained unresolved throughout 2023."], "start_unit": 2, "end_unit": 4, "locator": "extracted HTML block 2 through extracted HTML block 4", "exact_excerpt": "Economía\nAmérica Futura\nCiencia\nSalud\nTecnología\nCultura\nDeportes\nGente\n50 aniversario\nAtaques informáticos\nLos hackeos a instituciones públicas asedian al Gobierno de López Obrador\nEl Ejército, la Lotería Nacional, Pemex y la Secretaría de Comunicaciones han sido objeto de ataques cibernéticos en los que el Gobierno ha perdido información sensible\nBoris Zhitkov (Getty Images)\nZedryk Raziel\nMéxico -\n08 nov 2022 - 05:29\nCET\nCompartir en Whatsapp\nCompartir en Facebook\nCompartir en Twitter\nDesplegar Redes Sociales\nAñadir EL PAÍS en Google\nCompartir:\nWhatsapp\nFacebook\n\nTwitter\nBluesky\nLinkedin\nCopiar enlace\nEl hackeo a los servidores del Ejército mexicano por parte del colectivo Guacamaya significó la fuga de seis terabytes de\ninformación de inteligencia de extrema sensibilidad\n, en uno de los mayores ataques cibernéticos a una institución pública en México. Sin embargo, no ha sido la única vulneración de gran calado en la Administración de Andrés Manuel López Obrador, ni tampoco existen garantías de que un evento semejante no se repita. La Auditoría Superior de la Federación ha publicado informes en los que ha dado cuenta de carencias y fallas en los sistemas de ciberseguridad de varias instituciones que no han sido atendidas. Los diagnósticos corresponden en su mayoría al periodo del Gobierno de López Obrador, pues en sexenios anteriores no se elaboraban auditorías sobre la gestión de las tecnologías de información institucionales y la administración de riesgos.\nEl ciberataque más reciente afectó los servidores de la Secretaría de Infraestructura, Comunicaciones y Transportes (SICT) el pasado 24 de octubre. La dependencia publicó que un\nsoftware\nmalicioso infectó equipos de cómputo, lo que obligó a suspender sus sistemas para evitar vulneraciones graves y un posible robo de información. Como consecuencia de la infección, la SICT\nsuspendió los trámites\nde licencias y certificados del sector aéreo y autotransporte. La intrusión también provocó un retraso en los adiestramientos e incrementos salariales de los pilotos aviadores, de acuerdo con\nReforma\n.\nLa Auditoría Superior, el órgano encargado de comprobar cómo se gasta el dinero del erario asignado a las instituciones públicas, detectó desde 2019 que la SICT presentaba vulnerabilidades en materia de seguridad informática. “Se aplicó con deficiencias la metodología para el desarrollo de soluciones tecnológicas, lo cual propicia imperfecciones en la gestión de riesgos, el análisis de vulnerabilidades previo a la puesta en marcha de los aplicativos, las pruebas técnicas y funcionales, así como en el aseguramiento de la calidad de los sistemas”, señala la auditoría 346-DE.\nEl hackeo a la Secretaría de la Defensa Nacional (Sedena) puso al descubierto información delicada sobre el estado de salud del presidente Andrés Manuel López Obrador;\nreportes de inteligencia\nsobre cárteles de la droga; diagnósticos sobre la penetración de grupos delincuenciales en la capital de México y otras ciudades, y negociaciones bilaterales sobre\nextradiciones de narcotraficantes\na Estados Unidos. También reveló datos sobre violaciones a los derechos humanos cometidas dentro y por el Ejército, como el uso de\nprogramas de espionaje\npara vigilar a objetivos no criminales, como periodistas, activistas y funcionarios; el registro de denuncias de\nabusos sexuales\nno atendidas; o cifras del elevado\níndice de letalidad\n\nreportado en operativos castrenses.\nEn 2021, un año antes del\nhackeo de Guacamaya\n, la Auditoría señaló que la dependencia no había implementado medidas para identificar brechas de seguridad ni había corregido vulnerabilidades. Tampoco había llevado a cabo configuraciones necesarias en servicios de internet, correo electrónico y dispositivos extraíbles para limitar el tipo de información que podía ingresar o salir de la secretaría y para evitar la instalación de\nsoftware\nmalicioso en sus servidores y estaciones de trabajo. La Sedena tampoco implementó medidas básicas como supervisar que sus proveedores de tecnología efectuaran mantenimientos preventivos, instalaran actualizaciones o capacitaran al personal de la institución. Todas estas fallas, sostuvo la Auditoría, representaban un riesgo a la integridad, confidencialidad y disponibilidad de los sistemas de información, lo que ponía en riesgo, desde entonces, la operación de la Sedena, de acuerdo con el informe 68-GB.\nEn 2021, el grupo de ciberdelincuentes Avaddon\nsecuestró información de la Lotería Nacional\ne hizo pública una parte tras no haber obtenido el pago de un rescate de parte del Gobierno mexicano. El grupo de\nransomware\ndio a conocer que tenía en su poder documentos legales y financieros, contratos e información sobre un caso de acoso sexual dentro de la empresa paraestatal. La Auditoría Superior determinó que el hackeo se debió a que los servidores funcionaban con versiones del sistema operativo sin actualizaciones de seguridad. “Los servidores afectados por el ataque cibernético tenían versiones de sistemas operativos fuera del soporte del fabricante; además, ninguno contaba con soluciones para la prevención de pérdida de datos ni con las actualizaciones de seguridad (parches); asimismo, se identificaron deficiencias en la supervisión periódica de las actividades de las cuentas privilegiadas para detectar infracciones a las políticas de seguridad, así como transacciones irregulares en los servidores de misión crítica”, señaló el órgano fiscalizador en la auditoría 187.\nOtro ciberataque a gran escala afectó a la paraestatal Petróleos Mexicanos (Pemex) en noviembre de 2019, el primer año del Gobierno de López Obrador. Un grupo de hackers tomó el control de unos 6.000 equipos de los que se extrajeron 180 mil archivos. El ataque afectó la operación de las refinerías de Veracruz y Tabasco. La Auditoría determinó que en ese hackeo hubo responsabilidad de Pemex, más que del fabricante del sistema operativo (Microsoft), pues este corrigió la vulnerabilidad de los servidores expuestos seis meses antes del ataque. “Debido a la falta de gestión de actualizaciones de seguridad (parches), entre otros controles, la vulnerabilidad no fue remediada por Pemex, lo que contribuyó para que los equipos de cómputo hayan sido secuestrados, ocasionando la pérdida de activos de información en los servidores y equipos de usuario final, así como la interrupción de los procesos de negocio de la empresa”, se indicó en el informe 413-DE. El auditor detectó, además, que la paraestatal tenía equipos de cómputo con sistemas operativos obsoletos y que no contaban con soporte de parte del fabricante, “lo cual aumenta el riesgo para la seguridad de la información, debido a la falta de actualizaciones de seguridad para remediar vulnerabilidades que están expuestas a ataques cibernéticos”.\nSuscríbase aquí\na la\nnewsletter\nde EL PAÍS México y reciba todas las claves informativas de la actualidad de este país\nSobre la firma\nZedryk Raziel\nVer biografía\nReportero de EL PAÍS México, especialista en la cobertura de asuntos políticos y de corrupción. While the government does not generally impose restrictions on anonymity or encryption for internet users, investigative reporting has revealed some ongoing restrictions on users of the Tor network, a censorship circumvention tool.\nAn investigation conducted between 2020 and October 2023 found that the Mexican government blocks 39 URLs from 21 Mexican government agencies for users of the Tor network.\n1\nA representative from the president’s office claimed that the “protection measures,” which have reportedly been in place for at least 12 years, are necessary to block traffic that is “malicious, automated, or that could be a threat.” In response, a Tor representative stated that the government’s actions are a disproportionate and an “isolating” way to safeguard against potential cyber harms.\n2\nData from the Open Observatory of Network Interference (OONI) showed that Tor remained widely accessible in Mexico during the coverage period, indicating that there had not been wider efforts to restrict Tor itself (see B1).\n3\nPreviously, in April 2022,\n4\nthe SCJN struck down the creation of a problematic biometric cell phone registry that had been established through a reform to the Federal Telecommunications and Broadcasting Law passed by Congress in April 2021. The court echoed criticism from digital rights organizations in its ruling, citing the registry’s unnecessary infringement on privacy rights and inadequate data safeguards.\n\n5\nUsers would have been required to join the registry when purchasing a SIM card or activating a new prepaid mobile line.\n6\n1\nJacbo Nájera and Miguel Trujillo, “Gobierno mexicano y la red Tor [Mexican government and the Tor network],” GitHub, October 2023,\nhttps://github.com/tor-gob/reporte-mexico\n.\n2\nJacobo Nájera and Miguel Trujillo, “Mexican government faces criticism for blocking portions of the safe internet,” Global Voices, October 12, 2023,\nhttps://advox.globalvoices.org/2023/10/12/mexican-government-faces-crit…\n.\n3\nOONI, “OONI Measurement Aggregation Toolkit (MAT): Tor Test, Mexico,” OONI Explorer, accessed September 2024,\nhttps://explorer.ooni.org/chart/mat?probe_cc=MX&since=2023-09-16&until=…\n.\n4\nDarinka Rodríguez, “La Suprema Corte de México invalida la creación de un padrón de datos biométricos para usuarios de celulares” [Mexican Supreme Court invalidates the creation of a biometric registry for mobile phone users], El País, April 25, 2022,\nhttps://elpais.com/mexico/2022-04-25/la-suprema-corte-de-mexico-invalid…\n.\n5\n“Mexico’s president can prevent a privacy disaster: veto the new biometric mobile phone registry,” AccessNow, April 16, 2021,\nhttps://www.accessnow.org/mexicos-new-biometric-mobile-phone-registry/\n; Alessandro Mascellino, “Judge blocks Mexico’s biometric cell phone registry requirement,” Biometric Update, April 21, 2021,\nhttps://www.biometricupdate.com/202104/judge-blocks-mexicos-biometric-c…\n.\nC5\n1.00-6.00 pts\n0-6 pts\nDoes state surveillance of internet activities infringe on users’ right to privacy?\n1.00\n1\n6.00\n6\nThe government has used the poor security situation in the country to justify expanding the state’s surveillance powers, with little accountability and oversight. High-profile abuses of digital spying technologies meant for law enforcement purposes continued to emerge during the coverage period, but they have not been thoroughly investigated by authorities.\nIn February 2024, an investigation from R3D detailed the functions of the Cyberspace Operations Center (COC), a covert division of SEDENA created in 2016 to conduct “military operations in cyberspace.”\n1\nAccording to the investigation, the COC conducts social media monitoring of users who criticize the Mexican military or the federal government, at times creating inauthentic user accounts in an attempt to infiltrate trusted networks online and uncover more information about critical users. The investigation found that the COC uses a specialized software created by Israeli company WebintPro, called HIWIRE, to monitor social media activities and identify links between users, as well as to deploy bots to “exert soft influence on public opinion” (see B5).\n2\nThese activities are known through leaked documents;\n3\nthere is no legal basis for the operations of the COC in existing Mexican law and the contract that SEDENA signed to acquire HIWIRE has not been made publicly available.\n\nThe Mexican military is reported to be one of the world’s largest users of Pegasus spyware, which can surveil all activities on mobile devices with no apparent signs of a breach.\n4\nPegasus has been used to target those investigating government corruption and human rights abuses in Mexico.\n5\nA joint investigation by Citizen Lab and the Mexican civil society organization R3D revealed that two human rights defenders, Jorge Santiago Aguirre Espinosa and María Luisa Aguilar Rodríguez, both working at the Miguel Agustín Pro Juárez Human Rights Center (Centro PRODH), were targeted by Pegasus between June and September 2022. According to Citizen Lab, the alleged timing of the surveillance suggests that the military was involved in these cases, though it was not able to confirm this. At the time the spyware was apparently active on Aguirre and Aguilar’s devices, Centro PRODH had been investigating past human rights abuses committed by the Mexican Army and providing support for the relatives of those who were forcibly disappeared by the military.\n6\nIn October 2022, the #EjércitoEspía investigation, coordinated by several civil society organizations and media outlets, documented three additional cases of suspected military espionage using Pegasus. Human rights defender Raymundo Ramos and two journalists, Ricardo Raphael and one from the outlet Animal Político, were reportedly surveilled with Pegasus between 2019 and 2021.\n7\nThe evidence presented in the investigation, which includes forensic analysis by Citizen Lab, confirmed that the victims were targeted with zero-click exploits, which do not require any action from victims and are virtually undetectable.\n8\nAll three individuals had recently denounced human rights abuses by the Mexican armed forces, suggesting that the Mexican military was responsible for the illegal spying.\n9\nIn March 2023, the same organizations that produced the #EjércitoEspía investigation released new evidence about the surveillance of human rights defender Raymundo Ramos.\n10\nInternal documents obtained from SEDENA appeared to conclusively demonstrate that the military used Pegasus to spy on Ramos through the highly secretive Military Intelligence Center (CMI), which lacks any legal basis for spying on civilians. According to the investigation, the CMI operates under the command of military leadership and spied on Ramos in order to disrupt his journalistic investigation into the army’s human rights abuses.\n11\nIn May 2023, the\nNew York Times\nreported that Alejandro Encinas, the country’s undersecretary for human rights and a close ally of President López Obrador, had been targeted with Pegasus spyware. Encinas had recently investigated potential abuses by the military and had been publicly critical of the armed forces.\n12\nIn June, during the coverage period, the\nWashington Post\npublished an investigation reporting that historian Camilo Vicente Ovalle, who coordinates a truth commission about Mexico’s “Dirty War” as part of Encinas’ office, was also targeted with Pegasus in the latter half of 2022;\n13\na development which was condemned by the truth commission’s Historical Clarification Mechanism and by digital rights organizations.\n\n14\nPresident López Obrador denied the evidence presented by the organizations and the media outlets, repeatedly claiming that his government had not used Pegasus to spy on Encinas or other figures.\n15\nAfter the October 2022 #EjércitoEspía investigation was published, López Obrador dismissed claims that the government had spied on journalist Ricardo Raphael because “it would be a waste of time” to do so. He also claimed that the army does not spy, but rather does “intelligence.”\n16\nDespite these revelations, authorities have been slow to investigate, and past cases have continued to go unpunished.\n17\nIn January 2023, the INAI ordered SEDENA to carry out a thorough search and provide information related to the contracts that the military signed with the company Comercializadora Antsua, the exclusive supplier of the Pegasus spyware in Mexico.\n18\nAfter SEDENA refused to comply for more than a year, in June 2024, after the coverage period, a court ordered the military to turn over the documents.\n19\nThe government was first implicated in the use of Pegasus when the results of the Pegasus Project investigation were published in July 2021. Leaked data from NSO Group, originally accessed by Forbidden Stories and Amnesty International and shared with 15 media organizations, confirmed and expanded upon prior reporting on the Mexican government’s use of Pegasus against critical voices.\n20\nSome 15,000 phone numbers from a leaked list of 50,000—which are presumed to belong to the targets of NSO clients—belonged to people located in Mexico, including journalists and politicians; the Mexican bloc was the largest in the entire list.\n21\nIn November 2021, the Attorney General’s Office (FGR) announced its first detention in the Pegasus investigation: Juan Carlos García Rivera, an employee in one of the dozen private companies that served as intermediaries between the Mexican government and NSO Group. García Rivera was accused of spying on journalist Carmen Aristegui via Pegasus in a way that “affected, limited, and undermined her freedom of expression.”\n22\nIn January 2024, a judge in Mexico City acquitted García Rivera, determining that Aristegui had been spied on with Pegasus for her journalistic work but that prosecutors had failed to prove that García Rivera was directly responsible.\n23\nIn response, Article 19, R3D, and SocialTIC emphasized that actions seeking to ensure accountability must continue.\n24\nOfficials have access to other surveillance tools with interception capabilities. In April 2023, Citizen Lab reported that Israeli spyware vendor QuaDream had been contracted by several government clients, including Mexico, to provide surveillance technology.\n25\nLike Pegasus, QuaDream is reportedly a zero-click exploit. Previously, in May 2020, three civil society organizations found 21 active international mobile subscriber identity (IMSI)–catchers, which can be used to intercept mobile data from all devices in the immediate area, in central Mexico.\n26", "excerpt_sha256": "2a3b27809ff6bbee6612d5a377b50a2bc46d1164e90b0a4ad43240edc6312d18", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0017-E006", "source_id": "SRC-065783efb0b09db2", "url": "https://www.banxico.org.mx/publications-and-press/imf-article-iv-consultation-for-mexico/%7B44F43B6B-61BD-D795-4561-1601FDD3FDB0%7D.pdf", "title": "Mexico: 2022 Article IV Consultation—Press Release and Staff Report", "publisher": "International Monetary Fund", "raw_sha256": "8fac4146590dbe2ed458f7356748f5880d021e03cc4982b1e7efe083d33f622a", "fact_summary": "The administration's energy policy replaced auctions with nonmarket-based policies and expanded the state electricity company's regulatory role, creating conflicts of interest, contract disruption, and policy uncertainty; The USMCA trade agreement and its implementation remain important catalysts for\nhigher growth and reform.\n\n•    The agreement with the U.S. and Canada reduces regulatory divergence, complementing the\n     elimination of many tariffs under NAFTA. Implementation efforts include the establishment of\n     new, independent labor adjudication courts at the state level.\n\n•    An important challenge for Mexico is to increase the domestic value-added content of exports\n     to abide by the tighter rules of origin, including in the automotive and textile sectors. Meeting\n     these rules calls for complementary reforms, including through the structural fiscal reforms\n     discussed earlier (e.g., to support education and training).\n\n•    Mexico could also take steps to reduce trade barriers with other important partners, including\n     China, the EU, and Latin America. Strengthening customs cooperation could further increase\n     trade integration with neighboring Central America (Annex XIV).\n\n•    The implementation agenda should be complemented with regulatory moves fostering more\n     competition. High rates of firm creations suggest that the regulatory framework provides for a\n     low entry cost for businesses in many sectors. Yet regulatory hurdles in some sectors with critical\n     forward linkages (e.g., foreign direct investment restrictions in surface, maritime and air\n     transport) limit competition and, through the implications for input costs, harms the\n     competitiveness of other sectors. Also, cumbersome licenses and permits procedures create\n     vulnerabilities to corruption (e.g., road transport, custom brokerage and restaurants licenses,\n     construction permits). Streamlining licensing and permit procedures would reduce vulnerabilities\n     to corruption and improve competitiveness. Finally, the autonomous competition authority\n     should be strengthened by reversing recent budget cuts and preserving its statutory\n     independence.\n\n56.      Restoring a more predictable and independent energy policy would also boost\ncompetitiveness and investment. Actions to reverse the 2013 energy reforms remain incomplete,\nas Parliament rejected the change in the constitution needed for a full reversal, which has\n\n\n28    INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\nintroduced policy uncertainty about the way forward. A new electricity law, however, was approved\nand has replaced auctions by nonmarket-based policies, with regulatory power given to the\nComisión Federal de Electricidad (CFE), the state-owned electricity company. This has created\nconflicts of interest and disrupted contracts signed under the 2013 reform. Policies should instead\naim to encourage private sector participation in the energy sector. Restoring market-oriented\nregulatory frameworks would leverage Mexico’s large and diverse renewable energy resource base.\nIt would also provide for cheaper, more reliable, sustainable, and competitive energy supply to the\neconomy and people and secure the needed investment.\n\n57.      Mexico established a climate change mitigation and adaptation agenda early but\nreducing greenhouse gas emissions will require determined steps, including through\nambitious carbon pricing. 14 The expansion of the emission trading system (ETS) in 2023 will be an\nimportant step towards pricing emissions comprehensively. However, questions remain regarding\nthe scalability and parameters of the ETS, which is currently in a pilot phase covering a small number\nof large entities. Although these are responsible for around 40 percent of emissions, the ability to\nexpand coverage is not clear. Additionally, the size of the allocated allowances and the absence of\nlegally binding emissions caps mean that the market created by the pilot is limited. Ensuring\nadequate enforcement and monitoring of polluting activity, introducing legally binding emissions\ncaps, and introducing the planned auction system for allowances are key to making the ETS fully\nfunctional. Further, sectoral measures, such as feebates, public investment in clean energy\ninfrastructure networks, and regulatory reforms in the energy sector as discussed above could\nenhance the impact of carbon pricing.\n\nAuthorities’ Views\n\n58.      The authorities pointed to a sharp increase in public investment especially in\nunderdeveloped areas, to support growth and reduce regional disparities. They considered\nindustrial policy and strengthening human capital as major priorities to increase productivity. They\nagreed on the need to continue advancing the governance agenda and improve the effectiveness of\nthe anti-corruption and AML/CFT framework. They noted that their efforts to improve governance\nalso included greater reliance on the army to curb crime and the removal of conditionality in\ntransfer programs, the latter to lower the programs’ vulnerability to corruption. They were pleased\nthat the implementation of the 2019 labor law had already resulted in accelerated resolution of\nlabor disputes. The authorities were seeking to increase labor market formality with the outsourcing\nlaw and tax administration reforms. They considered that a regulatory framework strengthening\ntheir energy state-owned enterprises served the economy better.\n\n\nSTAFF APPRAISAL\n59.    Mexico is well placed to navigate a more challenging, potentially turbulent global\nenvironment. The post-pandemic recovery has been relatively gradual, but domestic inflation has\n\n14 A comprehensive climate mitigation strategy for Mexico is described in IMF WP/21/246.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                         INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND   29\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\naccelerated in line with developments elsewhere. Near-term growth prospects for the U.S. have\nweakened and global financial conditions have tightened as central banks have responded to high\ninflation, increasing the risks of capital flow reversals in emerging market economies. In this\nenvironment, Mexico will benefit from its very strong macroeconomic policies and policy\nframeworks but should also prepare contingency plans to react quickly to downside scenarios.\n\n60.      Growth is expected to slow in the near-term and the balance of risks is tilted to the\ndownside. After strong outturns in the first half of 2022, growth is projected to decline in the next\nfew quarters, while inflation is expected to gradually fall. More persistent global or domestic\ninflation, another spike in international oil or food prices, a greater-than-expected tightening of\nglobal financial conditions, or a sharper slowdown in U.S. growth are the main downside risks. On\nthe upside, an acceleration in nearshoring of production for better access to the North American\nmarket could moderate the impact of lower U.S. growth.\n\n61.      Tackling high inflation calls for further increases in the policy rate and maintaining it\nthere for some time. With significant uncertainty about the path for inflation in 2023 and important\nupside risks, a clearly restrictive policy stance for some time would be appropriate. Further steps in\nusing policy communication as a tool, including further information on the rate path consistent with\nBanxico’s inflation forecast, could reinforce the proactive approach to re-anchoring near-term\ninflation expectations and minimizing disruptions in financial markets.\n\n62.     The envisaged neutral fiscal stance in 2022 and 2023 is appropriate. With the economy\ncurrently operating at close to potential and restoring low and stable inflation a priority, a restrictive\nmonetary stance and broadly neutral fiscal stance is an appropriate policy mix.\n\n63.     The untargeted fiscal measures deployed to mitigate the impact of rising living costs\nraise efficiency concerns but have supported real incomes. Retail fuel price smoothing has\nreduced cost pressures for the economy but at a sizeable cost to the budget. Shifting to targeted\nsupport could safeguard priority spending and insulate the budget from swings in oil prices.\n\n64.      Fiscal reserves should be restored and the institutional framework for fiscal policy\nshould be strengthened. Rebuilding the FEIP stabilization fund to around 0.3 to 0.5 percent of GDP\nwould create room for fiscal policy to respond to near-term shocks. In the longer term, more fiscal\npolicy flexibility to respond to shocks could be achieved through the introduction of an explicit debt\nanchor with narrowly defined escape clauses and a clear mechanism to return to the debt path\nfollowing periods of deviation.\n\n65.     The floating exchange rate should continue to serve as a shock absorber. The economy\nis expected to remain resilient if downward pressure on the peso materializes, benefiting from deep\nFX markets and contained FX mismatches in balance sheets. In the event of large shocks to capital\nflows, monetary policy could be used to counter the potential inflationary impact of a weaker peso.\nFX intervention could be considered to mitigate a material worsening of market illiquidity.\n\n\n\n\n30   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\n66.      Additional measures would help the financial system remain resilient in a changing\nfinancial and regulatory landscape. While systemic vulnerabilities are found to be contained, the\n2022 FSAP flags the need for upgrading the financial sector oversight and crisis management\nframeworks to close some gaps and address emerging challenges. After aligning the legal and\nregulatory AML/CFT framework with Financial Action Task Force standards, the focus should now be\non strengthening the AML/CFT regime, including through adequate resourcing, ensuring the\navailability of high-quality beneficial ownership information, and monitoring emerging financial\nintegrity risks related to fintech.\n\n67.      Unlocking growth potential and reducing inequality require a broad supply side\nagenda. Despite increased trade openness and macroeconomic stability, productivity growth has\nbeen weak and the growth in output per worker has averaged close to zero over the past 15 years.\nThe authorities’ agenda addresses some obstacles to higher productivity and growth, but additional\nefforts are needed to address corruption, crime, and the weak rule of law; increase human capital\ninvestments and address infrastructure bottlenecks; and reduce labor and product market rigidities.\n\n68.      Financial inclusion should be deepened to support inclusive growth. The potential of\ndigital finance to increase financial access and depth should be fostered through increased\ncompetition in the financial sector, while maintaining safeguard to ensure financial stability and\nintegrity. Efforts to broaden digital connectivity and to improve the transparency of financial services\nshould continue.\n\n69.     Determined implementation of the anticorruption framework will be key to enhancing\nits effectiveness. Steps should include strengthening prevention, facilitating reporting including\nwhistleblower protection, and further empowering institutions in charge of investigation,\nprosecution, and oversight.\n\n70.      A gradual increase in productive government spending, financed by reforms to raise\ntax revenues, would promote growth and equity. Higher spending on education, health, public\ninvestment, and social protection is critical to improve human and physical capital and to narrow the\nsignificant variation in social outcomes across states. To be effective, this higher spending would\nneed to be accompanied by more efficient public spending, building on recent steps taken to\nimprove spending control and program design. Recent tax administration reforms have buoyed\nrevenue, but a well-designed tax policy reform is needed to reduce differences in VAT rates, reduce\ntax expenditures, and widen the top personal income tax bracket. These reforms should be\ncombined with adequate compensation to offset the impact on poorer households.\n\n71.     Recent labor market reforms should be adapted to lessen their negative effects on\nformal sector employment. The current strategy could be complemented by continued\nimplementation of the labor dispute resolution mechanisms; lowering firing restrictions; reducing\nthe regulatory costs of formalizing a business; and aligning increases in the real minimum wage to\nincreases in the productivity of lower wage workers.\n\n\n\n\n                                                                  INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND       31\n\nMEXICO\n\n\n\n72.     A more predictable energy policy that is more open to private sector participation\nwould boost competitiveness and investment. Reestablishing more market-oriented regulatory\nframeworks would leverage Mexico’s large and diverse renewable energy resource base. It would\nincentivize investments to create a cheaper, more reliable, sustainable, and competitive energy\nsupply.\n\n73.     Further steps toward carbon pricing would reduce greenhouse emissions. The\nexpansion in 2023 of the emission trading system (ETS) will be an important step towards\ncomprehensively pricing emissions. Ensuring adequate coverage, enforcement, and monitoring of\npolluting activity, introducing legally binding emissions caps, and introducing the planned auction\nsystem for allowances are key to making the ETS fully functional. Other measures, such as increasing\nthe carbon tax, introducing feebates, expanding public investment in clean energy infrastructure,\nand regulatory reforms could help Mexico achieve its NDCs.\n\n74.   It is recommended that the next Article IV consultation take place on the standard 12-\nmonth cycle.\n\n\n\n\n32   INTERNATIONAL MONETARY FUND", "excerpt_sha256": Organized in\npartnership with the Verification Centre of the German Armed Forces, the course covered a\nrange of topics, including international instruments, strategies, standards for arms and\nammunition management, and techniques in weapons and ammunition monitoring and\nverification. The participating officers were expected to play a pivotal role in bolstering SADC\nstrategies and operations to prevent the proliferation of illicit small arms and light weapons.\n\n\nThe SADC region reinforced efforts against small arms and light weapons proliferation\nthrough the active implementation of the SADC Protocol and alignment with the African\nUnion’s Master Roadmap of Practical Steps to Silence the Guns in Africa. In addition to\nobserving Africa Amnesty Month, the region actively participated in regional and interregional\njoint operations aimed at preventing and combating small arms and light weapons\nproliferation. Those coordinated efforts were crucial for enhancing regional stability, reducing\narmed violence, and ensuring the safety and security of SADC member States.\n\n\nAmericas\n\nOrganization of American States\n\nInter-American Convention against the Illicit Manufacturing of and Trafficking in Firearms,\nAmmunition, Explosives, and Other Related Materials\n\nIn 2023, the Technical Secretariat of the Inter-American Convention against the Illicit\nManufacturing of and Trafficking in Firearms, Ammunition, Explosives, and Other Related\nMaterials reported that 31 of the 34 OAS member States were States parties to that\nConvention.\n\n\nThe Convention’s Consultative Committee held its twenty-third regular meeting virtually on 5\nApril with Mexico as Chair. The Meeting also received support from the Convention’s Technical\nSecretariat, jointly staffed by the OAS Department of Public Security and the OAS Department\nagainst Transnational Organized Crime.\n\n\n\n\n                                               161\n\nCountering firearms trafficking\n\nAt the fifty-fourth meeting of the Group of Experts for the Control of Money-Laundering, held\non 23 and 24 May, delegations approved a study on best practices for coordination by\nauthorities in confiscating materials in cases of money-laundering used to support illicit arms\ntrafficking. The Working Sub-Group on Forfeiture and International Cooperation developed\nthe study with technical support from the OAS Department against Transnational Organized\nCrime.\n\n\nMeanwhile, through its Department of Public Security, OAS continued to provide its member\nStates with comprehensive assistance in strengthening their capacities against illicit firearms\nand ammunition proliferation and trafficking. Its assistance benefited from European Union\nfunds and emphasized legislative support, through-life management of weapons and\nammunition, armed violence prevention, and regional coordination mechanisms. In 2023, the\nDepartment of Public Security organized the delivery of capacity-building technical and\nmaterial assistance for weapons and ammunition management to 19 countries in Latin\nAmerica and the Caribbean. That assistance included training 1,011 national personnel,\nimproving security conditions in five storage areas, installing inventory control software in five\nnational defence and security institutions, and destroying 37,518 firearms and 110 tons of\nammunition. Additionally, a partnership with Spain enabled 11 military and police officers from\nseven Latin American countries to be certified in Level 3 explosive ordnance disposal.\n\n\nTo support efforts to prevent armed violence, the Department of Public Security supported\n1,100 at-risk youths, in Tela (Honduras) and in Lima, who participated in daily orchestra classes\naimed at improving their life skills. It also launched two massive open online courses on\npreventing armed violence and inaugurated the Center for Psychosocial Support of Victims of\nArmed Violence, in Tela.\n\n\nThe Department of Public Security also collaborated with the United Nations Regional Centre\nfor Peace, Disarmament and Development in Latin America and the Caribbean to launch the\ndevelopment of a Central American road map to prevent the illicit proliferation and trafficking\nof firearms and ammunition. With funding from the European Union, the Department of Public\nSecurity organized several rounds of awareness-raising and dialogue sessions with national\nauthorities, concluding with an in-person workshop to gather inputs for the future road map.\nThe anticipated plan was scheduled for adoption in 2024, with all Central American countries,\nexcept Nicaragua, engaging in the initiative.\n\n\nHumanitarian demining\n\nThe Department of Public Security’s comprehensive mine action programme continued to\nsupport Colombia in addressing explosive ordnance contamination, in line with a 2012\n\n\n\n                                                162\n\nbilateral agreement. In 2023, it supported quality management for seven humanitarian\ndemining operators, comprising two State/military entities and five humanitarian non-\ngovernmental organizations. The Department conducted seven evaluations of operational\nprocedures and 5,167 field monitoring visits, and it verified the decontamination of 314,587\nsquare metres of land.\n\n\nIn Colombia, the Department’s mine action programme delivered explosive ordnance risk\neducation directly to the public. Throughout the year, 11,689 people in 16 municipalities across\neight departments participated in regular explosive ordnance risk reduction activities. With\nrespect to age and gender, the group comprised 1,842 women, 1,592 men, 4,079 girls and\n4,176 boys.[9] Of those participants, 3,151 people in high-risk areas (447 women, 335 men, 1,173\ngirls and 1,196 boys) benefited from a special educational methodology emphasizing rapid\nresponse.[10] Furthermore, 20 survivors of explosives received assistance in accessing state\nmedical services and additional opportunities for sustainable livelihoods.\n\n\nSupport for the implementation of Security Council resolution 1540 (2004)\n\nIn 2023, the secretariat of the Inter-American Committee against Terrorism maintained its role\nin implementing Security Council resolution 1540 (2004) in the Americas, including through\nefforts to counter biological and chemical threats.\n\n\nThe Committee support ed eight countries[11] in strengthening biosafety and biosecurity\nmeasures in line with the resolution, emphasizing three priorities: (a) helping to align national\nlegislation with international standards; (b) conducting capacity-building activities, including\nbiosafety and biosecurity training of 250 technicians, scientists and laboratory personnel; and\n(c) undertaking outreach and awareness-raising, including through virtual events, to highlight\nbioterrorism and other related threats and establish a regional network of biosafety and\nbiosecurity professionals. The Committee also completed biosecurity threat assessments for\nfour countries and the broader region,[12] published four reports on its website, and analysed\nchemical weapons threats in key free trade zones, presenting the findings in international law\nenforcement forums.\n\n\nAs hemispheric coordinator for resolution 1540 (2004) in Latin America and the Caribbean, the\nCommittee facilitated two peer review exercises: (a) an exercise conducted in Mexico City in\nSeptember by Brazil, Chile and Mexico with a focus on biological weapons non-proliferation;\nand (b) an exercise in November and December between Ecuador and Paraguay on\nimplementing the Biological Weapons Convention. The Committee also collaborated with\nrelevant organizations to further strengthen the region’s network of points of contact for\nresolution 1540 (2004).\n\n\n\n\n                                               163", "excerpt_sha256": "b4e84695285fdf5c6e329c8445bdd166e1d086def9405151837167046aa80b4e", "verification_status": "corrected", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt supports Mexico’s chairmanship and participation in the peer-review exercise.", "The excerpt does not identify Mexico as one of the eight countries receiving biosafety and biosecurity support, so that attribution was narrowed out."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0020-E006", "source_id": "SRC-fc54d4bc672fbae5", "url": "https://digitallibrary.un.org/record/4063345/files/full-en-yb-vol-48-2023.pdf", "title": "United Nations Disarmament Yearbook 2023", "publisher": "United Nations Office for Disarmament Affairs", "raw_sha256": "97b8d04626a79634956b350167141e9e440de6ff091a1763a980137945ad98f4", "fact_summary": "Mexico updated its previously submitted assistance request to the Security Council 1540 Committee in 2023, while the Committee reviewed its assistance mechanism and conducted implementation workshops. Mexico also participated with Brazil and Chile in a peer-review exercise on resolution 1540 implementation held in Mexico City.", "question_ids": ["1B.6", "1B.9"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "The update and peer-review activity occurred in 2023, within the target period.", "ruler_attribution": "The source attributes the assistance-request update to Mexico as a state; it does not identify López Obrador’s personal role or the substance of the requested assistance.", "limitations": ["The source does not state what assistance Mexico requested, whether it was granted, or whether implementation improved.", "The peer-review activity is evidence of engagement, not proof of effective domestic safeguards."], "start_unit": 219, "end_unit": 221, "locator": "page 219 through page 221", "exact_excerpt": "duplication and overlap of such activities. Nuclear security focuses on the prevention and\ndetection of and response to criminal or intentional unauthorized acts involving or directed at\nnuclear material, other radioactive material, associated facilities or associated activities.\nResponsibility for nuclear security within a State rests entirely with that State, in accordance\nwith its respective national and international obligations.\n\n\nThe Agency continued to assist States upon request in their national efforts to establish and\nmaintain effective and comprehensive nuclear security of all nuclear and other radioactive\nmaterial at all times. These activities assist States’ efforts to establish effective and sustainable\nnational nuclear security regimes and, where appropriate, to fulfil their obligations, including\nunder the Convention on the Physical Protection of Nuclear Material and its Amendment, as\nwell as the relevant United National Security Council resolutions, such as resolution 1540\n(2004).\n\n\nImplementation of Security Council resolution 1540 (2004)\nIn resolution 1540 (2004), the Security Council decided that all States shall refrain from\nproviding any form of support to non-State actors that attempted to develop, acquire,\nmanufacture, possess, transport, transfer or use nuclear, chemical or biological weapons and\ntheir means of delivery, in particular for terrorist purposes. The Council required all States to\nadopt and enforce appropriate laws to that effect, as well as other effective measures to\nprevent the proliferation of those weapons and their means of delivery to non-State actors, in\nparticular for terrorist purposes.\n\n\nIn 2023, the Committee established pursuant to resolution 1540 (2004) (1540 Committee) was\nchaired by Hernán Pérez Loose from 31 January to 28 November, by Andrés Montalvo from 29\nNovember to 14 December, and by José De La Gasca from 15 to 31 December. In accordance\nwith resolution 2663 (2022), by which the Council extended the 1540 Committee’s mandate for\n10 years, until 30 November 2032, the Committee adopted its twentieth programme of work,\ncovering the period from 1 February 2023 to 31 January 2024 (S/2023/117, annex). The\nprogramme of work organized the Committee’s work around four pillars: (a) monitoring and\nimplementation; (b) assistance; (c) cooperation with international, regional and subregional\norganizations; and (d) transparency and outreach. The Office for Disarmament Affairs, the\nDepartment of Political and Peacebuilding Affairs, and the 1540 Committee’s Group of Experts\ncontinued to support the Committee in implementing its programme of work.\n\n\nNational implementation\n\nBy paragraph 4 of resolution 1540 (2004), the Security Council mandated States to submit a\nfirst report on the steps they had taken or intend to take to implement the resolution. By the\nend of 2023, 185 of the 193 Member States had submitted their first report, while 8 States had\n\n\n                                                 209\n\nyet to do so.[8] Furthermore, 124 Member States had submitted at least one additional report.\nAs of the end of the year, Member States had submitted a total of 537 first and additional\nnational reports on steps taken to implement resolution 1540 (2004).\n\n\nThe Security Council encouraged States in resolution 1977 (2011) and its successors to provide\nadditional information on their implementation efforts, including, voluntarily, on their laws and\nregulations and on States’ effective practices. In 2023, India, Türkiye and Uruguay submitted\nadditional information, bringing the number of States that had done so to 115.\n\n\nTo facilitate dialogue with the 1540 Committee on implementing resolution 1540 (2004), States\nare encouraged to submit up-to-date information on national points of contact, preferably\nboth in their capitals and at their respective permanent missions in New York. In 2023,\nresponding to renewed calls from the 1540 Committee, 43 States either named new or\nupdated existing points of contact, bringing the total number of States with designated points\nof contact for resolution 1540 (2004) to 146.\n\n\nThe Security Council encouraged States, by resolution 1977 (2011) and subsequent\nresolutions, to prepare, on a voluntary basis, national implementation action plans to map out\ntheir priorities and plans for implementing key provisions of resolution 1540 (2004). In 2023,\nMadagascar submitted such a plan, bringing the total, as at 31 December, to 47 voluntary\nnational implementation action plans, which were adopted by 38 States.\n\n\nThe Office for Disarmament Affairs supported several additional Member States in initiating\nprocesses to adopt voluntary national implementation action plans. In 2023, the Office\norganized workshops in Phnom Penh from 25 to 27 April, in Yaoundé from 12 to 14 September,\nin Bangkok from 10 to 12 October, in Abuja from 16 to 18 October, and in Vientiane from 28 to\n30 November. Those workshops convened various national ministries and agencies\nresponsible for implementing resolution 1540 (2004). The aim was to raise their awareness\nabout the resolution’s objectives and obligations, review the status of national\nimplementation, identify areas needing improvement, and establish priorities and targets for\nthe action plans.\n\n\nAssistance\n\nIn its resolution 2663 (2022), the Security Council reiterated that many Member States still\nrequired assistance in implementing resolution 1540 (2004) and emphasized the importance\nof improving the 1540 Committee’s matchmaking mechanism to ensure effective, timely and\ntailored assistance. The 1540 Committee initiated a review of its current assistance\nmechanism, established in 2018, including by conducting periodic reviews of pending\nassistance requests. In 2023, Iraq, Kenya, Kyrgyzstan and Madagascar submitted new\n\n\n\n\n                                                210\n\nassistance requests to the Committee. Additionally, Mexico and Zambia updated their\npreviously submitted requests from 2019 and 2020, respectively.\n\n\nCooperation with international, regional and subregional organizations\n\nIn resolution 1540 (2004) and its successors,[9] the Security Council recognized the need to\nenhance the coordination of efforts at the national, subregional, regional and international\nlevels to strengthen a global response to the threat from non-State actors. Likewise, the\nCouncil recognized the role and efforts of international, regional and subregional\norganizations, including United Nations bodies, in supporting Member States in their\nimplementation of resolution 1540 (2004), and called for continued cooperation in that regard.\n\n\nAccordingly, the Office of Disarmament Affairs engaged States and regional organizations to\nfoster and facilitate cooperation, coordination and exchanges at the subregional level. On 7\nand 8 March, in cooperation with Japan, a stakeholders meeting in Tokyo brought together\nrepresentatives from 14 States in the Asia-Pacific region.[10] In Africa, the Office collaborated\nwith Kenya and the Intergovernmental Authority on Development to convene a subregional\nworkshop on 14 and 15 March in Nairobi, drawing representatives from 7 States in the region.\n[11]\n       Members of the 1540 Committee also attended the regional workshops, which were\nintended to enrich States’ understanding of their obligations under resolution 1540 (2004) and\nthe status of its implementation in the respective subregions. The workshop offered States a\nplatform to share their experiences in implementing the resolution and discuss opportunities\nfor cross-border cooperation. Furthermore, Brazil, Chile and Mexico conducted, with the\nsupport of the Organization of American States, a peer-review exercise on the implementation\nof resolution 1540 (2004), which was held on 28 and 29 September in Mexico City.\n\n\nAt the level of the Security Council, the 1540 Committee continued to explore opportunities to\nenhance ongoing cooperation with the Security Council Committee established pursuant to\nresolutions 1267 (1999), 1989 (2011) and 2253 (2015) concerning Islamic State in Iraq and the\nLevant (Da’esh), Al-Qaida and associated individuals, groups, undertakings and entities. In\nparticular, the two Committees jointly briefed the Security Council in November (S/PV.9478).\n\n\nTransparency and outreach\n\nIn resolution 1540 (2004), the Security Council calls upon States to develop appropriate ways\nto work with and inform industry and the public regarding their obligations under national\nlaws and regulations adopted pursuant to the resolution and the key multilateral non-\nproliferation treaties. In support of those efforts, and within the context of the “Wiesbaden\nprocess” for industry outreach, the Office for Disarmament Affairs and Germany co-organized\na series of three global and regional conferences to engage and inform industry and the\nprivate sector. The conferences took place in Wiesbaden on 8 and 9 February, in Bangkok on\n\n\n\n                                                211", "excerpt_sha256": "51f71fd1e6bf3d7b1075b6882f956b1673619e940b93cc71b60f897d700fc704", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": ["The excerpt directly supports the 1540 Committee’s review of its assistance mechanism, Mexico’s update of its previously submitted assistance request, and the Brazil-Chile-Mexico peer-review exercise held in Mexico City on 28–29 September 2023."]}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0026-E020", "source_id": "SRC-26432e3d86aa471d", "url": "https://freedomhouse.org/country/mexico/freedom-net/2023", "title": "Mexico: Freedom on the Net 2023 Country Report", "publisher": "Freedom House", "raw_sha256": "55052483d1e8d22f7617e18dfe2b38b2b0aba2027783df80646eae8053c9bdea", "fact_summary": "During 2023 the INAI lacked a quorum and had nearly 6,000 pending cases after the Senate failed to appoint commissioners; López Obrador and allies supported dissolving the institution, while the Supreme Court later allowed it to meet with four commissioners. Existing law permitted extensive metadata retention and real-time geolocation access subject to judicial safeguards, and a 2023 biometric identity bill raised privacy concerns.", "question_ids": ["1B.9", "4B.4", "4B.9", "7B.6", "7B.7", "8B.10", "8B.2"], "polarity": "mixed", "period_fit": "Directly covers the 2023 reporting period and distinguishes executive-legislative obstruction from later judicial repair.", "ruler_attribution": "The report attributes support for dissolving INAI and the broader institutional posture to López Obrador and his allies; judicial restoration is attributed to the SCJN.", "limitations": ["The metadata law predates López Obrador and includes judicial-warrant requirements for historical data.", "The biometric identity bill was proposed or pending, not established as implemented policy in the extract."], "start_unit": 57, "end_unit": 60, "locator": "extracted HTML block 57 through extracted HTML block 60", "exact_excerpt": "6.00\n6\nArticle 189 of the 2014 Telecommunications Law forces companies to provide users’ geolocation information and other communications metadata to police, military, or intelligence agencies in real time. Article 190 requires providers to maintain records of their users’ metadata for a period of two years, and grants security agencies access to these records at any time.\n1\nIn 2016, the SCJN upheld the constitutionality of the law’s requirements for data retention and real-time geolocation. However, the ruling established the need for a judicial warrant to access historical metadata.\n2\nBeginning in March 2023 and through the end of the coverage period, the INAI, Mexico’s data protection authority and access to information body, was unable to function properly due to the lack of five-person quorum of commissioners. Despite a federal judge ordering the Senate to appoint a commissioner to at least one of the INAI’s three vacant seats,\n3\nthe Senate failed to do so at the end of May, after MORENA legislators accused the judiciary of interfering in its affairs.\n4\nAs of June, nearly 6,000 cases were awaiting resolution by the INAI.\n5\nAmidst the deadlock in May, Human Rights Watch called on the government to appoint commissioners, emphasizing that the INAI’s inability to function left Mexicans vulnerable to potential abuses of their personal data.\n6\nThe Senate’s failure to appoint the INAI commissioners comes amid calls from López Obrador and his allies to dissolve the body entirely and broader efforts by the president to undermine independent regulatory bodies (see A5).\n7\nIn August 2023, after the coverage period, the second chamber of the SCJN ruled that the INAI could meet with only four commissioners until a five-member quorum is appointed, allowing it to begin processing outstanding cases.\n8\nReforms to the criminal procedural code in 2016 required a judicial warrant for government entities to access geolocation data, with some exceptions, such as kidnapping cases in which a person’s life or physical integrity is in danger.\n9\nThe 2016 SCJN ruling also clarified which authorities can access user data; it included federal prosecutors, federal police, and the agency directly in charge of applying and coordinating the National Security Law.\nAn April 2021 reform of the Federal Telecommunications and Broadcasting Law established a biometric cell phone registry that tasked telecommunications companies with collecting biometric data; the SCJN ultimately ruled the registry unconstitutional in April 2022 (see C4).\n10\nThe database would have been accessible to authorities overseeing public security and justice.\n11\n\nDuring the coverage period, Congress again moved to create a biometric database that could undermine the right to privacy. A new bill that the Senate will examine, which was approved by the Chamber of Deputies in March 2023, would empower the Ministry of the Interior (SEGOB) to create and manage a centralized biometric database, the National Registry and Identity System (SID).\n12\nThe SID would require individuals to give biometric information to the SEGOB while registering activities at the Civil Registry, meaning that citizens would effectively have no means to consent to the collection of their personal biometric data.\n13\nCivil society organizations and some opposition lawmakers have raised concerns that the bill does not clearly specify which biometric data would be collected and which entities would have access to it, posing a risk to privacy and the protection of personal data.\n14\n1\nSee Articles 189-190 of Ley Federal de Telecomunicaciones y Radiodifusión.\n2\n“Inviolabilidad del contenido de las comunicaciones y de los datos que permitan identificarlas: segunda sala” [Inviolability of the or any other actor in relation to their online activities?\n0.00\n0\n5.00\n5\nJournalists for online outlets continue to face threats and violence from organized criminal groups, members of local governments, and other actors. Mexico remains the most dangerous country in Latin America for journalists and one of the most dangerous in the world.\n1\nArticle 19 documented 696 aggressions against journalists in 2022,\n2\nmaking it the most violent year for journalists since the organization started tracking in 2007. These figures include 181 instances of intimidation and harassment, 151 threats, 48 physical attacks, and 12 murders of journalists in potential relation to their work. Of all reported attacks against the press, 196 (28.19 percent) occurred in the digital sphere, including online intimidation and harassment, digital threats, and attempts to hack journalists' accounts.\n3\nNearly 49 percent of the total attacks against journalists related to coverage of corruption and politics, while 27.59 percent were linked to security and justice issues.\n4\nThe Committee to Protect Journalists (CPJ) separately reported that 13 journalists, including digital reporters,\n5\nwere killed in 2022, making it the deadliest year on record for journalists in Mexico.\n6", "excerpt_sha256": "ab20fc63ab6d9902748506cbd6a00d7515d8a6aa4e5bb3f971c0fed70f429c76", "verification_status": "accepted", "verification_notes": []}, {"evidence_id": "BATCH-0027-R01-E009", "source_id": "SRC-68ba67316f9e3122", "url": "https://documents1.worldbank.org/curated/en/588351544812277321/pdf/Mexico-Systematic-Country-Diagnostic.pdf", "title": "Mexico Systematic Country Diagnostic", "publisher": "World Bank", "raw_sha256": "5cfd27812cdf78b05980f54a4dfb13ab976beaf340028a2d22249f58b6bac2fb", "fact_summary": "Mexico had established climate-governance and disaster-finance instruments, including FONDEN, catastrophe insurance, a risk atlas, climate legislation, an NDC, a climate fund, and a carbon tax.
